Realization of Jordan-Kronecker invariants by Lie algebras
I. K. Kozlov

Abstract
We study what Jordan-Kronecker invariants of Lie algebras, introduced by A. V. Bolsinov and P. Zhang, are possible. We completely solve this problem in the Jordan and the Kronecker cases. We prove that any JK invariants that contain the Kronecker 3 × 3 3\times 3 block or several Kronecker 1 × 1 1\times 1 blocks are possible. For other JK invariants, with Kronecker indices k 1 , … , k q k_{1},\dots,k_{q} , we give a partial answer: • all Jordan–Kronecker invariants with no more than ∑ i k i \sum_{i}k_{i} Jordan tuples with multiple maxima are possible. • the Jordan–Kronecker invariants with more than ∑ i k i \sum_{i}k_{i} unique Jordan tuples with multiple maxima are impossible. We also desribe all JK invariants that can be realized by compatible Poisson brackets with non-constant eigenvalues.



1
Introduction

Jordan–Kronecker invariants of finite-dimensional Lie algebras were introduced in
[
5
]
. They arise as a combination of two simple facts:

•

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a finite-dimensional Lie algebra and
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be its dual space. Any element
x\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
defines a skew-symmetric bilinear form
\mathcal{A}_{x}=\left(c_{ij}^{k}x_{k}\right)
, where
c_{ij}^{k}
are the structural constants of
\mathfrak{g}
.

•

There is a canonical form for a pencil of skew-symmetric bilinear forms given by the Jordan–Kronecker theorem (Theorem
2.1
, see, e.g.,
[
24
,
9
]
).

The Jordan–Kronecker invariants of
\mathfrak{g}
are determined by the canonical form of the pencil
\mathcal{A}_{x+\lambda a}
for a generic pair
(x,a)\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}
(see Section
2.7
). In short, there are two kinds of blocks in the Jordan–Kronecker theorem: Kronecker blocks and Jordan blocks. The Jordan–Kronecker invariants of
\mathfrak{g}
are

•

sizes of Kronecker blocks,

•

sizes of Jordan blocks grouped by eigenvalues

for a generic pencil
\mathcal{A}_{x+\lambda a}
. The JK invariants of
\mathfrak{g}
are a collection of Jordan tuples and Kronecker sizes:

J_{\lambda_{1}}(2n_{11},\dots,2n_{1s_{1}}),\quad\dots,\quad J_{\lambda_{p}}(2n_{p1},\dots,2n_{ps_{p}}),\quad 2k_{1}-1,\quad\dots,\quad 2k_{q}-1.

(1)

Each
Kronecker size

2k_{j}-1
represent a Kronecker
(2k_{j}-1)\times(2k_{j}-1)
block. Each
Jordan tuple

J_{\lambda_{i}}(2n_{i1},\dots,2n_{is_{i}})
represent Jordan blocks with sizes
2n_{i1}\times 2n_{i1},\dots,2n_{is_{i}}\times 2n_{is_{i}}
and the same eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
. In (
1
) we regard
\lambda_{i}
as (distinct) formal variables, since the eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
depend on
(x,a)
. Nevertheless, the number and sizes of blocks are the same for a generic pair
(x,a)\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}
.

We always assume that the numbers in the Jordan tuples
J_{\lambda_{i}}(2n_{i1},\dots,2n_{is_{i}})
are sorted in the descending order:

n_{i1}\geq n_{i2}\geq\dots\geq n_{is_{i}}.

We say that a Jordan tuple
has a unique maximum
, if
n_{i1}>n_{i2}
or
s_{i}=1
. Otherwise, if
n_{i1}=n_{i2}
, then the Jordan tuple
has multiple maxima
. A Jordan tuple
J_{\lambda_{i}}\left(2n_{i1},\dots,2n_{is_{i}}\right)
is
unique
, if

\left(2n_{i1},\dots,2n_{is_{i}}\right)\not=\left(2n_{j1},\dots,2n_{js_{j}}\right),\qquad i\not=j.

Apart from
[
5
]
and references therein, the Jordan–Kronecker invariants for various Lie algebras were calculated in
[
29
]
,
[
30
]
,
[
31
]
,
[
32
]
,
[
10
]
,
[
11
]
. Yet the following question, asked in
[
1
]
,
[
2
]
and
[
4
]
remains open.

Question 1.1
.

What Jordan–Kronecker invariants can be realized by complex finite-dimensional Lie algebras?

We completely solve this question in the pure Kronecker and pure Jordan case, when there is only one type of blocks. In the mixed case we give a partial answer. Here is a short summary of our results:

1.

(Kronecker case) Any collection of Kronecker sizes
2k_{1}-1,\dots,2k_{q}-1
is possible (Theorems
3.1
and
3.4
).

2.

(Jordan case) A collection of Jordan tuples
J_{\lambda_{i}}(2n_{i1},\dots 2n_{is_{i}})
,
i=1,\dots,p
, is possible if and only if each of them has a unique maximum, i.e.
n_{i1}>n_{ij}
(Theorem
4.1
).

3.

(Mixed case, “Positive result”) All JK invariants with Kronecker
3\times 3
block or several
1\times 1
blocks are possible (Theorems
6.1
and
7.1
).

4.

(Mixed case,
1\times 1
Kronecker block) A collection of Jordan tuples
J_{\lambda_{i}}(2n_{i1},\dots 2n_{is_{i}})
,
i=1,\dots,p
, and one Kronecker
1\times 1
block is possible if and only there is no more than
1
Jordan tuple with multiple maxima, i.e.
n_{i_{0}1}=n_{i_{0}2}\geq n_{i_{0}j}
for some
i_{0}\in\left\{1,\dots,i\right\}
(Theorem
6.1
).

5.

(General mixed case) Consider the remaining mixed case, i.e. there are no Kronecker
3\times 3
blocks and no more than one
1\times 1
Kronecker block. Assume that the Kronecker sizes are
2k_{1}-1,\dots,2k_{q}-1
.

(a)

(“Positive result”) All JK invariants that have no more than
k_{1}+\dots+k_{q}
Jordan tuples with multiple maxima are possible (Theorem
7.6
).

(b)

(“Negative result”) The JK invariants that have more than
k_{1}+\dots+k_{q}
unique Jordan tuples with multiple maxima are impossible (Theorem
9.4
).

6.

(1 Kronecker block, no semi-invariants) Let
\mathfrak{g}
be Lie algebra without (proper) semi-invariants
1
1

1

\mathfrak{g}
has no (proper) semi-invariants if its radical is nilpotent, see Remark
10.19
. It is also well-known that if
\mathfrak{g}
is perfect, i.e.
[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]=\mathfrak{g}
, then its radical is nilpotent and, hence,
\mathfrak{g}
has no semi-invariants.
and with only one Kronecker block (i.e.
\operatorname{ind}\mathfrak{g}=1
), the size of the Kronecker block is
(2k-1)\times(2k-1)
. Either there are no Jordan tuples, or there are
k
equal Jordan tuples (see Theorem
10.20
).

The JK invariants of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
are the invariants of its Lie–Poisson pencil (see Section
2.8
). For any Poisson pencil
\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right\}
on a manifold
M
we can define the JK invariants and algebraic type of the pencil
\mathcal{P}
at each point
x\in M
(see Section
2.6
). Simply speaking, if the JK invariants are

J_{\lambda_{1}(x)}(2n_{11},\dots,2n_{1s_{1}}),\quad\dots,\quad J_{\lambda_{p}(x)}(2n_{p1},\dots,2n_{ps_{p}}),\quad 2k_{1}-1,\quad\dots,\quad 2k_{q}-1,

then the algebraic type is the multiset

\left\{(2n_{11},\dots,2n_{1s_{1}}),\quad\dots,\quad(2n_{p1},\dots,2n_{ps_{p}}),\quad 2k_{1}-1,\quad\dots,\quad 2k_{q}-1\right\}.

Obviously, the algeraic type is the same in a neighbouhood of a generic point
x\in M
. Instead of Question
1.1
we can ask the following more general question.

Question 1.2
.

What JK invariants can be realized by compatible Poisson brackets with non-constant eigenvalues?
More formally, let
\mathcal{P}
be a Poisson pencil on a manifold
M
such that

1.

the algebraic type of
\mathcal{P}
at all points
x\in M
is the same,

2.

d\lambda_{i}(x)\not=0
, where
\lambda_{i}(x)
are the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{P}
.

What is the algebraic type of
\mathcal{P}
(at any point
x\in M
)?

Remark 1.3
.

Obviously, we can realize all JK invariants by constant Poisson brackets. Therefore we add the condition
d\lambda_{i}(0)\not=0
. This is a simple natural condition that is satisfied for the Lie–Poisson pencils (Lemma
4.13
) and is the main obstruction for realization of JK invariants in the Jordan case (Section
4.2
).

Simply speaking, Question
1.2
asks: what are differential-geometric obstructions to realization of JK invariants? It turns out that there is only one obstruction: in the Jordan case (i.e. when there are no Kronecker blocks) each Jordan tuple must have a unique maximum. This follows from Turiel’s theorems about compatible symplectic structures (see Section
4.2
). In the Jordan case the JK invariants are realized in Theorem
4.1
. As stated above, for Lie algebras there are other obstructions in the mixed case. But for Poisson pencils with non-constant eigenvalues all JK invariants with at least one Kronecker block are possible.

Theorem 1.4
.

For any collection (i.e. multiset) of tuples and numbers

\left\{(2n_{11},\dots,2n_{1s_{1}}),\dots,(2n_{p1},\dots,2n_{ps_{p}}),\quad 2k_{1}-1,\dots,2k_{q}-1\right\}

(2)

with at least one number (i.e.
q\geq 1
) there exists a a manifold
M
and a Poisson pencil
\mathcal{P}
on it such that:

1.

the JK invariants of
\mathcal{P}
at all points
x\in M
are the Jordan tuples
J_{\lambda_{i}(x)}(2n_{i1},\dots,2n_{is_{i}})
,
i=1,\dots,p
and the Kronecker sizes
2k_{j}-1
,
j=1,\dots,q
,

2.

all eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}(x)
are distinct and
d\lambda_{i}(x)\not=0
for any point
x\in M
.

Remark 1.5
.

Theorem
1.4
formally holds if there are no Jordan tuples, i.e.
p=0
.

We prove Theorem
1.4
in Section
8.5
. We cannot realize some JK invariants by Lie algebras, because the local structure of Poisson pencils sometimes clashes with the properties of Lie algebras. The restrictions on JK invariants of Lie algebras, that we discuss in this paper, mainly come from the following
2
important results of F. J. Turiel about the local structure of Poisson pencils:

•

Local form for nondegenerate Poisson pencils (=compatible symplectic structures),
[
25
]
(see Section
4.2
and Appendix
A
).

•

Local decomposition of Poisson pencils into the Jordan–Kronecker product,
[
26
]
(see Theorem
6.9
and Appendix
B
).

Also, in this paper we prove several interesting results that can be useful in a general study of Poisson pencils or Lie algebras:

•

In Section
5
we introduce some useful local coordinates for Poisson pencils
\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}
(Theorems
5.9
and
5.12
).

•

In Lemma
9.8
we prove a well-known statement that eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}(x)
of a Poisson pencil
\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}
satisfy

(\mathcal{A}-\lambda_{i}(x)\mathcal{B})d\lambda_{i}(x)=0

We also prove a similar statement for semi-invariants of Lie algebras in Lemma
9.11
.

All Lie algebras are finite-dimensional and complex (although the results about Lie algebras remain true for an arbitrary algebraically closed field
\mathbb{K}
with
\operatorname{char}\mathbb{K}=0
). For a manifold
M
there are two cases:

•

M
is complex, and all the functions, Poisson brackets, differential forms, etc. are complex analytic and holomorphic.

•

M
is real
\mathbb{C}^{\infty}
-smooth, and all the functions, Poisson brackets, differential forms, etc. are
\mathbb{C}^{\infty}
-smooth.

Since we study complex Lie algebras, all the statements hold in the complex case. The majority of statements hold in the real
\mathbb{C}^{\infty}
-case, and we specify if a statement doesn’t hold or has to be modified in the real case
2
2

2

In the real case we often need to add the condition that all the eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
are real. The case of complex conjugate eigenvalues
\lambda_{\pm}=\alpha\pm i\beta
often can be dealt with, since there is a natural almost complex structure
J
. The construction of such structure
J
is discussed, e.g. in
[
25
]
,
[
17
]
or
[
3
]
. We don’t bother with the complex conjugate eigenvalues in this paper.
. We try to state results in such a way that they hold in both complex and real case. In the complex case by “smooth” we mean complex analytic and holomorphic.

All the manifolds are connected. Moreover, the statements about manifolds are local. Therefore, without loss of generality, one may consider all manifolds to be open disks in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
(or in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
in the real case). We say that some property holds for a
generic
point of a manifold
M
, if it holds on an open dense subset of
M
. For short, we use JK as an abbreviation of Jordan-Kronecker.

Acknowledgements.
The author would like to thank A. V. Bolsinov and A. M. Izosimov for useful comments.

2
Basic definitions

For completeness sake we briefly recall the definition of JK invariants and some important facts about them. For details, see
[
5
]
.

2.1
Jordan–Kronecker theorem

First, let us recall the canonical form for a pair of skew-symmetric forms.

Theorem 2.1
(Jordan–Kronecker theorem,
[
24
]
).

Let
A
and
B
be skew-symmetric bilinear forms on a
finite-dimension vector space
V
over a field
\mathbb{K}
with
\textmd{char }\mathbb{K}=0
. If the field
\mathbb{K}

is algebraically closed, then there exists a basis of the space
V

such that the matrices of both forms
A
and
B
are block-diagonal
matrices:

A=\begin{pmatrix}A_{1}&&&\\
&A_{2}&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&A_{k}\\
\end{pmatrix}\hskip 8.50012ptB=\begin{pmatrix}B_{1}&&&\\
&B_{2}&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&B_{k}\\
\end{pmatrix}

where each pair of corresponding blocks
A_{i}
and
B_{i}
is one of
the following:

•

Jordan block with eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{K}
:

A_{i}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}0&\begin{matrix}\lambda_{i}&1&&\\
&\lambda_{i}&\ddots&\\
&&\ddots&1\\
&&&\lambda_{i}\\
\end{matrix}\\
\hline\cr\begin{matrix}\hbox{-}\lambda_{i}&&&\\
\hbox{-}1&\hbox{-}\lambda_{i}&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&\hbox{-}1&\hbox{-}\lambda_{i}\\
\end{matrix}&0\end{array}\right)\hskip 8.19447ptB_{i}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}0&\begin{matrix}1&&&\\
&1&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&1\\
\end{matrix}\\
\hline\cr\begin{matrix}\hbox{-}1&&&\\
&\hbox{-}1&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&\hbox{-}1\\
\end{matrix}&0\end{array}\right)

(3)

•

Jordan block with eigenvalue
\infty

A_{i}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}0&\begin{matrix}1&&&\\
&1&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&1\\
\end{matrix}\\
\hline\cr\begin{matrix}\hbox{-}1&&&\\
&\hbox{-}1&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&\hbox{-}1\\
\end{matrix}&0\end{array}\right)\hskip 8.19447ptB_{i}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}0&\begin{matrix}0&1&&\\
&0&\ddots&\\
&&\ddots&1\\
&&&0\\
\end{matrix}\\
\hline\cr\begin{matrix}0&&&\\
\hbox{-}1&0&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&\hbox{-}1&0\\
\end{matrix}&0\end{array}\right)

(4)

•

Kronecker block

A_{i}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}0&\begin{matrix}1&0&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&1&0\\
\end{matrix}\\
\hline\cr\begin{matrix}\hbox{-}1&&\\
0&\ddots&\\
&\ddots&\hbox{-}1\\
&&0\\
\end{matrix}&0\end{array}\right)\hskip 8.19447ptB_{i}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}0&\begin{matrix}0&1&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&0&1\\
\end{matrix}\\
\hline\cr\begin{matrix}0&&\\
\hbox{-}1&\ddots&\\
&\ddots&0\\
&&\hbox{-}1\\
\end{matrix}&0\end{array}\right)

(5)

A proof of the Jordan–Kronecker theorem can be found in
[
24
]
,
which is based on
[
9
]
.

Remark 2.2
.

A Jordan
\infty
-block is a Jordan
0
-block, where the matrices
A_{i}
and
B_{i}
are swapped.

Remark 2.3
.

Each Kronecker block is a
(2k_{i}-1)\times(2k_{i}-1)
block, where

k_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
. If
k_{i}=1
, then the blocks are
1\times 1

zero matrices

A_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}0\end{pmatrix}\quad B_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}0\end{pmatrix}

Remark 2.4
.

Note that in
[
5
]
the matrix
B_{i}
for Jordan blocks is taken with the opposite sign. This is a question of convention — whether
\lambda_{i}
or
-\lambda_{i}
should be called an eigenvalue. Here is the difference:

1.

In this paper the Jordan block with eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
is given by

A_{i}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&J_{\lambda_{i}}\\
-J_{\lambda_{i}}^{T}&0\end{matrix}\right),\qquad B_{i}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&E\\
-E&0\end{matrix}\right).

Then the matrix of recursion operator consists of 2 blocks with eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
:

P_{i}=B_{i}^{-1}A_{i}=\left(\begin{matrix}J_{\lambda_{i}}^{T}&0\\
0&J_{\lambda_{i}}\end{matrix}\right).

But the rank of
A+\lambda B
drops for
minus
\lambda_{i}
:

\operatorname{rk}\left(A-\lambda_{i}B\right)<\max_{\lambda}\,\,\operatorname{rk}\left(A+\lambda B\right).

2.

In
[
5
]
the Jordan block with eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
is given by

A_{i}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&J_{\lambda_{i}}\\
-J_{\lambda_{i}}^{T}&0\end{matrix}\right),\qquad B_{i}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&-E\\
E&0\end{matrix}\right).

Then the rank drops for
\lambda_{i}
:

\operatorname{rk}\left(A+\lambda_{i}B\right)<\max_{\lambda}\,\,\operatorname{rk}\left(A+\lambda B\right).

But the matrix of recursion operator consists of 2 blocks with eigenvalue
minus
\lambda_{i}
:

P_{i}=B_{i}^{-1}A_{i}=\left(\begin{matrix}-J_{\lambda_{i}}^{T}&0\\
0&-J_{\lambda_{i}}\end{matrix}\right).

We chose the first variant, so that a pair of forms
A,B
and the recursion operator
P=B^{-1}A
would have the same eigenvalues. Also,
B_{i}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&E\\
-E&0\end{matrix}\right)
means that the basis for a Jordan block is a Darboux basis for the symplectic form
B_{i}
, which is “more natural”. We pay for it with “an ugly minus” in
\operatorname{Ker}(A-\lambda_{i}B)
. Oh well, one cannot get all the pluses.

2.1.1
Linear Poisson pencils

Let
A
and
B
be skew-symmetric forms on a complex finite-dimensional vector space
V
. We use the following notations:

A_{\lambda}=A+\lambda B,\qquad A_{\infty}=B.

Let us intoduce several definitions.

•

We call the one-parametric family of skew-symmetric forms

\mathcal{P}=\left\{A+\lambda B\,\,\bigr|\,\,\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}\right\}

a
linear Poisson pencil
on
V
. Note that the parameter
\lambda
of the pencil belongs to the complex projective line
\lambda\in\bar{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{CP}^{1}=\mathbb{C}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
.

•

A vector space with a linear Poisson pencil on it
(V,\mathcal{P})
is a
bi-Poisson vector space
. We may denote
(V,\mathcal{P})
by
V
if the pencil is clear from the context. A subspace of a bi-Poisson space
W\subset(V,\mathcal{P})
is considered with the induced pencil
\mathcal{P}\bigr|_{W}
on it.

•

The
rank
of a pencil
\mathcal{P}
is

\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}=\max_{\lambda\in\bar{\mathbb{C}}}\,\operatorname{rk}A_{\lambda}.

•

A form
A_{\lambda}
is
regular
if
\operatorname{rk}A_{\lambda}=\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}
.

•

The eigenvalues of Jordan blocks
\lambda_{i}
are also called
characteristic numbers
of the pencil
\mathcal{P}
. The set of characteristic numbers
\lambda_{i}
of the pencil
\mathcal{P}
is denoted by

\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}=\left\{\lambda\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}\quad\bigr|\quad\operatorname{rk}A_{-\lambda}<\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}\right\}.

In other words, a form
A_{-\lambda}=A-\lambda B
is regular if and only if
\lambda\not\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
.

•

We call a decomposition of
V
into a sum of subspaces corresponding to Jordan and Kronecker blocks a
Jordan-Kronecker decomposition
:

\left(V,\mathcal{P}\right)=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N}\left(V_{J_{\lambda_{j},2n_{j}}},\mathcal{P}_{J_{\lambda_{j},2n_{j}}}\right)\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{q}\left(V_{K_{i}},\mathcal{P}_{K_{i}}\right).

(6)

Since the pencils on the subspaces
V_{J_{\lambda_{j},2n_{j}}}
and
V_{K_{i}}
are induced by
\mathcal{P}
, we often omit them and denote the JK decomposition (
6
) as

V=\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N}V_{J_{\lambda_{j},2n_{j}}}\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{q}V_{K_{i}}\right).

–

\left(V_{J_{\lambda_{j},2n_{j}}},\mathcal{P}_{J_{\lambda_{j},2n_{j}}}\right)
corresponds to a Jordan
2n_{j}\times 2n_{j}
block with eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}
and is called a
Jordan subspace
with Jordan index
2n_{j}
and eigenvalue
\lambda
.

–

\left(V_{K_{i}},\mathcal{P}_{K_{i}}\right)
corresponds to a Kronecker
\left(2k_{i}-1\right)\times\left(2k_{i}-1\right)
block and is called a
Kronecker subspace
with Kronecker index
k_{i}
.

•

A basis of a Jordan or a Kronecker subspace is a
standard basis
if the forms
A
and
B
are as in the JK theorem (i.e. they have the form (
3
), (
4
) or (
5
)).

The JK decomposition is not unique, just as the basis in the JK theorem. But the sizes and types of blocks in the JK theorem are uniquely defined.

2.2
Core and mantle subspaces

2.2.1
Definition of the core and the mantle

Denote by
V_{J}
and
V_{K}
the sum of all Jordan and all Kronecker subspaces respectively:

V_{J}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N}V_{J_{\lambda_{j},2n_{j}}},\qquad V_{K}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{q}V_{K_{i}}.

The decomposition

V=V_{J}+V_{K}

for a linear pencil
A+\lambda B
is not as natural as it seems. Rather, intuitively speaking, the Jordan part
V_{J}
is “sandwiched” between “the greater and lesser halfs” of Kronecker blocks. In order to formalize this intuition, let us introduce two important invariantly defined subspaces, that we will need below.

Definition 2.5
.

Consider a pencil of skew-symmetric forms
\left\{A_{\lambda}=A+\lambda B\right\}
.

1.

The
core
subspace
3
3

3

This neat terminology is due to A. V. Bolsinov.
is

K=\sum_{\lambda-regular}\operatorname{Ker}A_{\lambda}.

2.

The
mantle
subspace is the skew-orthogonal complement to the core (w.r.t. any regular form
A_{\mu}
)

M=K^{\perp}.

Now fix any basis from the JK theorem and let us describe the core and mantle subspaces in it. First, let us describe
\operatorname{Ker}A_{\lambda}
for each type of block.

Proposition 2.6
.

1.

For one Jordan block with eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}

A+\lambda B=\left(\begin{matrix}0&J_{\lambda_{0}+\lambda}\\
-J_{\lambda_{0}+\lambda}^{T}&0\end{matrix}\right),\qquad J_{\mu}=\left(\begin{matrix}\mu&1&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&&\\
&&\ddots&1\\
&&&\mu\end{matrix}\right)

we have
\operatorname{Ker}(A+\lambda B)=0
unless
\lambda=-\lambda_{0}
. The same holds if
\lambda_{0}=\infty
.

2.

And for one Kronecker block

A+\lambda B=\left(\begin{matrix}0&P\\
-P^{T}&0\end{matrix}\right),\qquad P=\left(\begin{matrix}1&\lambda&&\\
&1&\ddots&&\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&&1&\lambda\end{matrix}\right)

with a standard basis
e_{1},\dots,e_{k-1},f_{1},\dots,f_{k}
we have

\operatorname{Ker}\left(A+\lambda B\right)=\operatorname{span}\left(f_{k}-\lambda f_{k-1}+\lambda^{2}f_{k-2}-\dots\right).

(7)

As a consequence we get another easy description of the core and mantle.

Corollary 2.7
.

For any JK decomposition (
6
) we have the following.

1.

The core subspace
K
of
V
is the direct sum of core subspaces of Kronecker subspaces
V_{K_{i}}
:

K=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{q}\left(K\cap V_{K_{i}}\right).

If
e_{1},\dots,e_{k_{i}-1},f_{1},\dots,f_{k_{i}}
is a standard basis of
V_{K_{i}}
, then the core subspace of
V_{K_{i}}
is

K\cap V_{K_{i}}=\operatorname{span}\left(f_{1},\dots,f_{k_{i}}\right).

2.

The mantle subspace
M
is the core plus all Jordan blocks:

M=K\oplus V_{J}.

Simply speaking, the core subspace
K
is spanned by the basis vectors corresponding to the down-right zero matrices of Kronecker blocks, like this one:

A_{i}+\lambda B_{i}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}0&\begin{matrix}1&\lambda&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&1&\lambda\\
\end{matrix}\\
\hline\cr\begin{matrix}\hbox{-}1&&\\
\hbox{-}\lambda&\ddots&\\
&\ddots&\hbox{-}1\\
&&\hbox{-}\lambda\\
\end{matrix}&\pagecolor{blue!25}0\end{array}\right).

Corollary
2.7
shows the following:

1.

the mantle subspace
M
is correctly defined;

2.

the core
K
is a
bi-isotropic
subspace (i.e. it is isotropic w.r.t. any regular form
A+\lambda B
);

3.

the restrictions of
A,B
to
M/K
are well-defined.

Remark 2.8
.

Now, the analogy with the Earth in Fig.
1
becomes obvious. The mantle
M
“wraps around” the core
K
, the rest of Kronecker blocks is “kind of a crust”.

Figure 1
:
Earth structure.

For those who are interested in this “entertaining geology”, the mantle and the core also have some natural structures, although we do not need them. For example:

1.

The core
K
has layers, namely, sums of “bigger half” of Kronecker blocks with size not greater than
(2k-1)\times(2k-1)
. Invariantly, each such layer is generated by polynomial solutions of

(A+\lambda B)v(\lambda)=0

with
\deg v(\lambda)<k
.

2.

The quotient
M/K\approx V_{J}
naturally decomposes into the sums of Jordan blocks corresponding to different eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
:

V_{J}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{p}V_{J_{\lambda_{i}}},\qquad V_{J_{\lambda_{i}}}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{s_{i}}V_{J_{\lambda_{i},2n_{ij}}}.

2.2.2
Kernels for linear Poisson pencils

Below, we need several simple corollaries from Proposition
2.6
about linear independence of vectors
v_{i}\in\mathrm{Ker}\,\,A_{\lambda_{i}}
. For one Kronecker block
\mathrm{Ker}\,\,A_{\lambda}
is given by (
7
) and we get the following.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
A+\lambda B
be the
(2k-1)\times(2k-1)
Kronecker block. Then non-zero vectors
v_{i}\in\mathrm{Ker}\,\,A_{\lambda_{i}}
for
M
distinct values
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}
are linearly independant if and only if
M\leq k
.

Recall that a
(2k-1)\times(2k-1)
Kronecker block has Kronecker index
k
. In general case we get the following.

Proposition 2.10
.

Consider a linear pencil
A+\lambda B
on a vector space
V
.

1.

\operatorname{Ker}A_{\lambda}\cap\operatorname{Ker}A_{\mu}
, for any
\lambda\not=\mu
, is the sum of Kronecker subspaces with Kronecker index
1
.

2.

If
v_{i}\in\operatorname{Ker}A_{\lambda_{i}}
, where
i=1,\dots,M
and
\lambda_{i}\not=\lambda_{j}
, are linearly dependent, then all
v_{i}
belong to the sum of Kronecker subspaces with Kronecker indices
k_{j}<M
.

Subspaces from Proposition
2.10
can also be described as follows:

•

\operatorname{Ker}A_{\lambda}\cap\operatorname{Ker}A_{\mu}
corresponds to the sum of trivial
1\times 1
Kronecker blocks.

•

Kronecker subspaces with Kronecker indices
k_{j}<M
correspond to the sums of Kronecker blocks with size not bigger than
(2M-3)\times(2M-3)
.

Proof of Proposition
2.10
.

1.

Easily follows from the JK theorem.

2.

Let
A_{\lambda}
be a regular form.
\mathrm{Ker}\,\,A_{\lambda}
is the sum of its intersection with Kronecker blocks:

\mathrm{Ker}\,\,A_{\lambda}=\mathrm{Ker}\,\,A_{\lambda}\cap\bigoplus_{i=1}^{q}V_{K_{i}}.

Let
\sum_{i}c_{i}v_{i}=0
. Without loss of generality all
c_{i}\not=0
. The same equality must hold for each Kronecker block. By Proposition
2.9
if the Kronecker index
k_{j}\geq M
, then the components of all
v_{i}
in this Kronecker subspace must be zero vectors. Thus, all
v_{i}
belong to the sum of Kronecker subspaces with Kronecker indices
k_{j}<M
, as required.

Proposition
2.10
is proved. ∎

2.3
Jordan-Kronecker invariants

Definition 2.11
.

The
Jordan-Kronecker invariants
of a pair of skew-symmetric bilinear forms
A
and
B
are

•

sizes of Kronecker blocks,

•

eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
, and sizes of Jordan blocks for each eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
.

Thus, we represent every Kronecker
(2k-1)\times(2k-1)
block with its
Kronecker size

2k-1
(the number
k
is its
Kronecker index
). For each eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
the corresponding Jordan blocks with sizes
2n_{i1}\times 2n_{i1},\dots,2n_{is_{i}}\times 2n_{is_{i}}
are represented by the corresponding
Jordan tuple

J_{\lambda_{i}}(2n_{i1},\dots,2n_{is_{i}})
. The JK invariants are the collection of all Jordan tuples and Kronecker sizes.

Example 2.12
.

Assume that in the JK theorem the blocks for forms
A
and
B
are

•

one Jordan
4\times 4
block with eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}
,

•

two Jordan
2\times 2
blocks with eigenvalue
\lambda_{2}\not=\lambda_{1}
,

•

two
1\times 1
Kronecker blocks,

•

one
3\times 3
Kronecker block,

•

and one Kronecker
5\times 5
block.

Then the JK invariants for
A
and
B
consist of
2
Jordan tuples and
4
Kronecker sizes:

J_{\lambda_{1}}(4),\qquad J_{\lambda_{2}}(2,2),\qquad 1,\qquad 1,\qquad 3,\qquad 5.

(8)

The Kronecker indices (i.e. halves of Kronecker sizes rounded up) are

k_{1}=1,\qquad k_{2}=1,\qquad k_{3}=2,\qquad k_{4}=3.

Remark 2.13
.

The notation (
8
) is not ideal, since it does not empasize that the numbers are Kronecker sizes. We suggest to denote the tuple (i.e. a sequence) of Kronecker sizes as

K(2k_{1}-1,\dots,2k_{q}-1).

Then, in practice, we can write JK invariants (
8
) as

J_{\lambda_{1}}(4),\qquad J_{\lambda_{2}}(2,2),\qquad K(1,1,3,5).

Remark 2.14
.

A Kronecker
(2k-1)\times(2k-1)
block can be represented by any of the following 3 numbers:

•

Kronecker size
2k-1
,

•

Kronecker index
k
,

•

minimal index
m=k-1
.

It is a question of convention — which number to choose for the JK invariants. We use the Kronecker sizes and always write them as
2k-1
. To avoid confusion between the indices
k
,
m=k-1
and the dimension
2k-1
, we may specify the size
(2k-1)\times(2k-1)
of the Kronecker block. For instance, we may say “JK invariants with a Kronecker
(2k-1)\times(2k-1)
block”.

Remark 2.15
.

In terms of
[
9
]
(and
[
24
]
):

•

Kronecker indices are
minimal indices
plus 1,

•

halves of sizes of Jordan blocks are
degrees of elementary divisors
4
4

4

More precisely, each Jordan
2n\times 2n
block with eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}
corresponds to a pair of elementary divisors
\left\{(\lambda-\lambda_{0})^{n},(\lambda-\lambda_{0})^{n}\right\}
, see also Lemma
2.17
.
.

There is not much difference between Kronecker indices and minimal indices. Kronecker indices
k_{i}
appear as bounds to the degrees of
\operatorname{Ad}^{*}
-invariant polynomials:

\operatorname{deg}f_{i}\geq k_{i},

in A. S. Vorontsov’s theorem from
[
28
]
.

The characteristic numbers (i.e. eigenvalues)
\lambda_{i}
are invarians of pencils
A+\lambda B
. But we don’t need their exact values for the JK invariants. We only care whether
\lambda_{i}
are equal or not for different Jordan blocks. This will be useful for compatible Poisson brackets, when characteristic numbers
\lambda_{i}(x)
depend on the point
x
.

2.3.1
Algebraic type of a pencil

Below, when considering Poisson pencils on manifolds, we work with linear Poisson pencils
\mathcal{P}_{x}
that depend on a paramater
x
. Ofther the number and sizes of all blocks are the same but the eigenvalues depend on the parameter
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i}(x)
. It is convenient to formalize the notation of JK invariants that are equal“up to exact values of eigenvalues”.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a linear Poisson pencil on a vector space
V
and let

J_{\lambda_{1}}\left(2n_{11},\dots,2n_{1s_{1}}\right),\quad\dots,\quad J_{\lambda_{p}}\left(2n_{p1},\dots,2n_{ps_{p}}\right),\quad 2k_{1}-1,\quad\dots,\quad 2k_{q}-1.

(9)

be its JK invariants.

Definition 2.16
.

The
algebraic type
of a linear Poisson pencil
\mathcal{P}
is the multiset of tuples and numbers

\operatorname{Type}\left(\mathcal{P}\right)=\left\{\left(2n_{11},\dots,2n_{1s_{1}}\right),\quad\dots,\quad\left(2n_{p1},\dots,2n_{ps_{p}}\right),\quad 2k_{1}-1,\quad\dots,\quad 2k_{q}-1\right\}.

Each tuple
\left(2n_{i1},\dots,2n_{is_{i}}\right)
is an ordered sequence, where

n_{i1}\geq n_{i2}\geq\dots\geq n_{is_{i}}.

Note that
\operatorname{Type}\left(\mathcal{P}\right)
is a multiset, i.e. multiple instances of tuples and numbers are allowed. A tuple with one element
(2n)
and the number
2n
are distinguished. For example, the following four algebraic types are pairwise different:

\left\{(2,2),2\right\},\qquad\left\{(2),(2),2\right\},\qquad\left\{(2),2,2\right\},\qquad\left\{2,2,2\right\}.

Two linear Poisson pencils
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
have the same algebraic type if and only if they have the same Kronecker sizes and there is one-to-one correspondence between their eigenvalues such that the sizes of Jordan blocks for any corresponding characteristic numbers are the same.

2.4
Computation of Jordan-Kronecker invariants

Let us state some simple facts that will help us to check that the Lie algebars that we construct below have the desired JK invariants. We provide only some basic techniques to calculate JK invariants, for more information about them see, e.g.
[
5
]
.

2.4.1
Jordan blocks

We start with Jordan blocks. First, if B is nondegenerate
\operatorname{Ker}B=0
, then we can consider the
recursion operator

P=B^{-1}A
. There are no Kronecker blocks, and each Jordan block of
A,B
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}
corresponds to a pair of Jordan blocks of the recursion operator
P=B^{-1}A
with the same eigenvalue:

A_{i}+\lambda B_{i}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&J_{\lambda_{0}+\lambda}\\
-J_{\lambda_{0}+\lambda}^{T}&0\end{matrix}\right)\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad P_{i}=B_{i}^{-1}A_{i}=\left(\begin{matrix}J_{\lambda_{0}}^{T}&0\\
0&J_{\lambda_{0}}\end{matrix}\right).

The general case can be easily reduced to the nondegenerate case, since the sum of Jordan blocks for
A,B
is the quotient of the mantle by the core

V_{J}\approx M/K

and if
B
is a regular form, then the restriction
B\bigr|_{M/K}
is nondegenerate.

Lemma 2.17
.

Assume that
B
is a regular form of a linear pencil
\mathcal{P}=\left\{A+\lambda B\right\}
on
V
. Take any
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Consider two numbers:

•

N
is the number of Jordan
2n\times 2n
block with eigenvalue
\lambda
for the Jordan–Kronecker decomposition of
A,B
.

•

M
is the number of Jordan
n\times n
blocks with eigenvalue
\lambda
in the Jordan canonical form (JCF) of the recursion operator
P=\left(B\bigr|_{M/K}\right)^{-1}A\bigr|_{M/K}
.

Then
M=2N
.

In
[
5
]
the
characteristic polynomial

p_{\mathcal{P}}(\lambda)
of
\mathcal{P}=\left\{A+\lambda B\right\}
is defined as follows. Consider all diagonal minors
\Delta_{I}
of the matrix
A+\lambda B
of order
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{P}
and take the Pfaffians
\operatorname{Pf}(\Delta_{I})
, i.e. square roots, for each of them. The characteristic polynomial is the greatest common divisor of all these
Pffaffians:

p_{\mathcal{P}}(\lambda)=\operatorname{gcd}\left(\operatorname{Pf}(\Delta_{I})\right).

The next statement follows from Lemma
2.17
.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\left\{A+\lambda B\right\}
be a linear pencil and let

•

p_{\mathcal{P}}(\lambda)
be the characteristic polynomial of
\mathcal{P}
,

•

\det(P-\lambda I)
be the characteristic polynomial of the recursion operator
P=\left(B\bigr|_{M/K}\right)^{-1}A\bigr|_{M/K}
.

Then
\det(P-\lambda I)=p_{\mathcal{P}}(\lambda)^{2}
.

Note that the degree of the characteristic polynomial of
\mathcal{P}
is

\dim p_{\mathcal{P}}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}V_{J}.

Below we use the following simple statement.

Proposition 2.19
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\left\{A+\lambda B\right\}
be a linear pencil on
V
with core
K
, mantle
M
and characteristic polynomial
p_{\mathcal{P}}(\lambda)
. Then

\operatorname{dim}K=\dim V-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}-\deg p_{\mathcal{P}},\qquad\operatorname{dim}M=\dim V-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}+\deg p_{\mathcal{P}}.

In all the examples below the core
K
, the mantle
M
and the recursion operator
P
will be quite easy to find. In general, this may be a nontrivial task.

Remark 2.20
.

Sizes of Jordan blocks can also be defined as elementary divisors through g.c.d. of minors of
A+\lambda B
(or Pfaffians of minors, since everything is skew-symmetric). See
[
9
]
for more details.

2.4.2
Kronecker blocks

If Jordan tuples are, roughly speaking, “one half of the Jordan canonical form of the recursion operator
P
”, the Kronecker blocks are all about “the spread” of
\operatorname{Ker}\left(A+\lambda B\right)
for various
\lambda
and the polynomial solutions of

\left(A+\lambda B\right)v(\lambda)=0.

(10)

Let us denote the set of all its polynomial solutions as
\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{P}
(i.e.
\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{P}\subset\mathbb{C}[\lambda]
). Recall that we know the structure of
\operatorname{Ker}A_{\lambda}
for each form
A_{\lambda}
from Proposition
2.6
. As a consequence we get the following 2 propositions.

Proposition 2.21
.

The number of Kronecker blocks for a linear pencil
\mathcal{P}
on
V
is

\dim V-\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}=\dim\operatorname{Ker}A_{\lambda},\qquad\forall\lambda\text{ - regular}.

Proposition 2.22
.

For each Kroncker block consider its corresponding polynomial solution
v_{i}(\lambda)
of (
10
) from Proposition
2.6
. Then
v_{i}(\lambda)
are minimal degree generators of the
\mathbb{C}[\lambda]
-module of all polynomial solutions of (
10
):

\mathbb{C}[\lambda]v_{1}(\lambda)+\dots+\mathbb{C}[\lambda]v_{\operatorname{corank}\mathcal{P}}(\lambda)=\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{P}.

Hence, we can find the Kronecker indices as in
[
9
]
by iteratively taking solutions
x_{1}(\lambda),x_{2}(\lambda),\dots
of (
10
) so that at each step:

•

x_{i}(\lambda)
are linearly independent,

•

\operatorname{deg}x_{i}(\lambda)
is as small as possible.

Then the Kronecker indices are
\operatorname{deg}x_{i}(\lambda)+1
. We also have the following simple statement, which is a slight generalization of Corollary 2 from
[
5
]
.

Corollary 2.23
.

Let
k_{1}\leq k_{2}\leq\dots\leq k_{q}
be the Kronecker indices of a linear Poisson pencil
\mathcal{P}=\left\{A+\lambda B\right\}
on
V
. Suppose that
N
polynomials

v_{i}(\lambda)=\sum_{j=0}^{m_{i}}v_{i}^{(j)}\lambda^{j},\qquad v_{i}^{(j)}\in V,\quad i=1,\dots,N,

are solutions of (
10
) such that their initial vectors
v_{i}(0)
are linearly independant. Assume that the numbers
m_{i}=\operatorname{deg}v_{i}(\lambda)
are ordered so that
m_{1}\leq m_{2}\leq\dots\leq m_{N}
. Then

m_{i}\geq k_{i},\qquad i=1,\dots,\min\left(q,N\right).

If this way to calculate Kronecker indices turns out to be too hard, sometimes the next corollary from Proposition
2.6
can help.

Proposition 2.24
.

Take a sufficiently large number of distinct regular values
\lambda_{0},\dots,\lambda_{N}\in\mathbb{C}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
. Denote

m_{k}=\dim\left(\sum_{i=0}^{k}\operatorname{Ker}A_{\lambda_{i}}\right).

Then
m_{j+1}-m_{j}
is the number of Kronecker blocks with size greater than
(2j+1)\times(2j+1)
. Thus, the number of Kronecker indices
k_{i}
equal to
j
is

2m_{j}-m_{j+1}-m_{j-1}.

Here, formally,
m_{-1}=0
.

2.5
Poisson manifolds

For the definition and basic facts about Poisson brackets see any standard book on Poisson manifolds, e.g.
[
7
]
,
[
23
]
. One of the most important facts about Poisson brackets is the well-known Darboux-Weinstein theorem (or Weinstein splitting theorem).

Theorem 2.25
(Darboux–Weinstein theorem).

Let
x
be a point of rank
2k
of a Poisson manifold
(M^{n},\mathcal{A})
. There exist local coordinates
(p^{1},\dots,p^{k},q^{1},\dots,q^{k},z^{1},\dots,z^{n-2k})
such that the Poisson bracket is given by

\{p^{i},q^{j}\}=\delta^{i}_{j},\qquad\{z^{i},z^{j}\}=\varphi^{ij}(z^{1},\dots,z^{n-2k}),

where all other brackets of coordinate functions are zero and
\varphi^{ij}(z(x))=0
.

In other words, the Poisson bivector has the form

\mathcal{A}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{i}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{i}}+\sum_{i<j}\varphi^{ij}(z)\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}

and the corresponding matrix is

\left(\mathcal{A}^{ij}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}0&I_{k}&0\\
-I_{k}&0&0\\
0&0&\Phi(z)\end{matrix}\right),\qquad\Phi(z(x))=0.

Remark 2.26
.

If
\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{A}=2k
in a neighbourhood of
x
, then
\Phi(z)\equiv 0
and the Poisson structure is locally flat

\{p^{i},q^{j}\}=\delta^{i}_{j},\qquad\{z^{i},z^{j}\}=0.

(11)

Two functions
f
and
g

commute
or are
in involution
w.r.t. a Poisson bracket if
\{f,g\}=0
. A function
f
is a
Casimir function
of a Poisson bracket if
\left\{f,g\right\}=0
for any function
g
. Note that
z^{i}
are local Casimir functions of the Poisson bracket (
11
).

2.5.1
Symplectic leaves.

Any Poisson manifold
(M,\mathcal{A})
is a union of symplectic manifolds. For any point
x\in(M,\mathcal{A})
the image
\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{A}_{x}
is called the
characteristic space
at x of the Poisson structure
\mathcal{A}
. In the local coordinates from Theorem
2.25
the characteristic space is

\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{A}_{x}=\operatorname{span}\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{1}},\quad\dots,\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{k}},\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{1}},\quad\dots,\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{k}}\right\}.

For short we denote the characteristic foliation
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\left(T^{*}M\right)
as
\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{A}
.

Definition 2.27
.

An
integral submanifold
for
\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{A}
is a path-connected submanifold
S\subseteq M
satisfying
T_{x}S=\mathcal{C}_{x}
for all
x\in S
. Integral submanifolds of
\mathcal{A}
are regularly immersed symplectic manifolds with the symplectic structure given by

\omega_{S}(X_{f},X_{g})(x)=-\left\{f,g\right\}(x).

Maximal integral submanifolds of
\mathcal{A}
are called the
symplectic leaves
of
\mathcal{A}
.

The symplectic leaves of the bracket (
11
) (locally) are the level surfaces of the Casimir functions:

S=\left\{z^{j}=\operatorname{const},\quad j=1,\dots,n-2k\right\}.

2.6
Compatible Poisson brackets

Definition 2.28
.

Two Poisson structures
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are
compatible
if any their linear combination
\alpha\mathcal{A}+\beta\mathcal{B}
with constant coeffitients is also a Poisson structure.

If
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are compatible Poisson structures, then we denote

\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}=\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B},\qquad\mathcal{A}_{\infty}=\mathcal{B}.

We call a one-parametric family of compatible Poisson brackets

\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\,\,\bigr|\,\,\lambda\in\mathbb{K}\cup\infty\right\}

a
Poisson pencil
or a pencil of Poisson brackets. Let
\mathcal{P}=\left\{A_{\lambda}\right\}
be a Poisson pencil on
M
. The
rank
of
\mathcal{P}

\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}=\max_{\lambda}\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}.

Similarly, the rank of
\mathcal{P}
at a point
x\in M
is the maximal rank of
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
at the point
x
. A bracket
A_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}
is
regular
at a point
x
if

\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}(x)=\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}(x).

Below we use the following trivial fact.

Proposition 2.29
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}\right\}
be a Poisson pencil on
M
. If
\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}=2k
in a neighbourhood of
x\in(M,\mathcal{P})
and a bracket
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
is regular at
x
, then
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
is regular in a sufficiently small neighbourhood of
x
.

2.6.1
Regular points for compatible Poisson brackets

Let
\mathcal{P}=\left\{A_{\lambda}=\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right\}
be a Poisson pencil on
M
. At each point
x\in M
we have a pair of
2
-forms on the cotangent bundle:

\mathcal{A}_{x},\mathcal{B}_{x}\in\Lambda^{2}(T_{x}M).

The
JK invariants
of
\mathcal{P}
at
x\in M
are the JK invarians of the linear Poisson pencil
\mathcal{A}_{x}+\mathcal{B}_{x}
.

Definition 2.30
.

A point
x_{0}\in(M,\mathcal{P})
is
JK-regular
in a neighbourhood of
Ox_{0}
the pencils
\mathcal{P}_{x}
have the same algebraic type.

Simply speaking, locally the JK invaraints are “equal up to eigenvalues” (see Definition
2.16
). In other words,
x_{0}\in(M,\mathcal{P})
is JK-regular if in a neighbourhood of
x_{0}
there exists a local frame
v_{1}(x),\dots,v_{n}(x)
such that the matrices of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
have the block-diagonal form as in the JK theorem, but the eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}(x)
depend on
x\in M
:

A_{i}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}0&J(\lambda_{i}(x))\\
\hline\cr-J^{T}(\lambda_{i}(x))&0\end{array}\right),\quad B_{i}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}0&E\\
\hline\cr-E&0\end{array}\right).

(12)

Note that for JK-regular points the number of distinct eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}(x)
locally remains the same. Eigenvalues that are equal at
x_{0}
remain equal in a neighbourhood
Ox_{0}
:

\lambda_{i}(x_{0})=\lambda_{j}(x_{0})\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\lambda_{i}(x)=\lambda_{j}(x),\quad x\in Ox_{0}.

2.7
Jordan–Kronecker invariants of Lie algebras

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a finite-dimensional complex Lie algebra and
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be its dual space. Fix a basis of
\mathfrak{g}
and denote by
c^{i}_{jk}
the structure constants of
\mathfrak{g}
in that basis. For a pair of points
x,a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
consider the skew-symmetric forms

\mathcal{A}_{x}=\left(\sum_{i}c^{i}_{jk}x_{i}\right),\qquad\mathcal{A}_{a}=\left(\sum_{i}c^{i}_{jk}a_{i}\right)

(13)

and the linear Poisson pencil

\mathcal{P}_{x,a}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}\right\}

generated by them. There exists a non-empty Zariski open subset

U\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}

such that the algebraic type of the pencil
\mathcal{P}_{x,a}
is the same for all
(x,a)\in U
. Recall that we defined the algebraic type as a multiset in Definition
2.16
. Denote the algebraic type of
\mathcal{P}_{x,a}
for
(x,a)\in U
as

\operatorname{JK}(\mathfrak{g})=\left\{\left(2n_{11},\dots,2n_{1s_{1}}\right),\,\,\dots,\,\,\left(2n_{p1},\dots,2n_{ps_{p}}\right),\,\,2k_{1}-1,\,\,\dots,\,\,2k_{q}-1\right\}.

(14)

In other words, the JK invariants of a pair of skew-symmetric form
\mathcal{A}_{x}
and
\mathcal{A}_{a}
(given by Definition
2.11
) have the form

J_{\lambda_{1}}\left(2n_{11},\dots,2n_{1s_{1}}\right),\quad\dots,\quad J_{\lambda_{p}}\left(2n_{p1},\dots,2n_{ps_{p}}\right),\quad 2k_{1}-1,\quad\dots,\quad 2k_{q}-1.

(15)

We say that
(x,a)\in U
is a
JK-generic pair
. The corresponding pencil
\left\{\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}\right\}
is called
JK-generic
too.

Definition 2.31
.

The
Jordan-Kronecker invariants
of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
are

•

sizes of Kronecker blocks,

•

sizes of Jordan blocks grouped by eigenvalues

for the JK-generic pencil
\mathcal{P}_{x,a}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}\right\}
, given by (
13
).

In other words, the JK invariants of
\mathfrak{g}
are the numbers
2k_{j}-1
and the tuples
\left(2n_{i1},\dots,2n_{is_{i}}\right)
. The algebraic type of a generic pencil
\operatorname{JK}(\mathfrak{g})
, given by (
14
), consists of the JK invariants of
\mathfrak{g}
.

Remark 2.32
.

It is possible to denote the JK invariants of
\mathfrak{g}
as (
14
). Yet, we use the notation
15
and regard
\lambda_{i}
as distinct formal variables. We find the expression (
15
) more descriptive. In particular, it emphasizes that Jordan tuples correspond to different characteristic numbers
\lambda_{i}
. Also, we often want to work with the JK invariants of JK-generic pencils
\mathcal{P}_{x,a}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}\right\}
. Roughly speaking,

“JK invariants of
\mathcal{P}_{x,a}
= JK invariants of
\mathfrak{g}
+ eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}(x,a)
”.

The eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i}(x,a)
, which are locally analytic functions for JK-generic pairs
(x,a)
, often come in handy. So, we need the notation (
15
) anyway.

Remark 2.33
.

Note the difference between Definitions
2.11
and
2.31
.

•

The JK invariants of
a pair of forms

A,B
contain eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
. The eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
are values in
\mathbb{CP}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
.

•

The JK invariants of
a Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}
have no eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
. Jordan tuples correspond to distinct eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
, but they “do not remember” their exact values. Each JK-generic pencil
\left\{\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}\right\}
has eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i}(x,a)
, but they depend on the pair
(x,a)
.

Remark 2.34
.

It seems like the definition of JK invariants is not well established yet. Different authors may change it slightly.

•

In
[
5
]
the algebraic type of a generic pencil
\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}
was called the JK invariant of
\mathfrak{g}
. Note that the word “invariant” is singular, not plural here. In our notations this is the multiset (
14
). This definition, where an “invariant” is a “type”, is valid but slightly counter-intuitive. The Kronecker sizes are also “invariants” of
\mathfrak{g}
. It is a question of preference, but the multiset (
14
) is rather a “collection of invariants”, then an “invariant”.

•

In
[
2
]
the JK invariants are Kronecker indices
k_{i}
and half-sizes of Jordan blocks
n_{ij}
for each eigenvalue. This definition is very close to Definition
2.31
. We use the term “tuple” to group sizes of Jordan blocks together. Unlike
[
2
]
, we use sizes of blocks and not halves of sizes. Otherwise, it would be a hassle to keep in mind that a Jordan
2\times 2
block is given by the index
1
, and that the Kronecker
3\times 3
block is given by the index
2
. All Jordan blocks are even-dimensional and all Kronecker blocks are odd-dimensional — much easier to remember!

•

In
[
29
]
,
[
30
]
,
[
31
]
and
[
32
]
the JK invarians are the sizes of blocks. This is not ideal, there is no need to lose the valuable information — which Jordan blocks have the same eigenvalue. It is better to group Jordan sizes in tuples and not “lump them together”.

It seems like usually by the JK invariants various authors mean the discrete invariants, i.e. the sizes of Jordan and Kronecker blocks (which they describe “in various shape or form”). What they probably mean is the multiset (
14
), but the lack of terminology and notations hindered the progress a little bit.

2.7.1
Index of a Lie algebra

For Lie algebras we can also describe the number Kronecker blocks as follows.

•

Let
a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Denote by
\mathfrak{g}_{a}
the the stationary subalgebra
of
a
in the sense of the coadjoint representation:

\mathfrak{g}_{a}=\left\{\xi\in\mathfrak{g}\,\,\bigr|\,\,\operatorname{ad}^{*}_{\xi}a=0\right\}.

•

In terms of the Lie–Poisson pencil

\mathfrak{g}_{a}=\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{A}_{a}.

•

The index of Lie algebra is

\operatorname{ind}\mathfrak{g}=\min_{x\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}}\dim\mathfrak{g}_{x}.

•

The index of
\mathfrak{g}
is the number of functionally independent local analytic coadjoint invariants (i.e., Casimir functions).

Proposition 2.35
(A. V. Bolsinov, P. Zhang,
[
5
]
).

Consider JK invariants of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. The number of Kronecker blocks is equal to
\operatorname{ind}\mathfrak{g}
.

2.7.2
Characteristic numbers and singular set

Let us recall some facts from
[
5
]
about Jordan blocks and characteristic numbers for the pencils
\left\{\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}\right\}
, given by (
13
). For a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, existence and structure of Jordan invariants are closely related to the structure of the
singular set

\operatorname{Sing}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Or, more precisely, to the union
\operatorname{Sing}_{0}
of its codimension 1 components.

Proposition 2.36
(A. V. Bolsinov, P. Zhang,
[
5
]
).

Consider the pencil
\left\{\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}\right\}
, given by (
13
). Then
\lambda_{i}
is its characteristic number iff
x-\lambda_{i}a\in\operatorname{Sing}
.

Remark 2.37
.

See Remark
2.4
why we take
x-\lambda_{i}a
and not
x+\lambda_{i}a
.

The singular set
\operatorname{Sing}\subset\mathfrak{g}
is an algebraic variety given by the
system of homogeneous polynomial equations of the form:

\operatorname{Pf}C_{i_{1},\dots,i_{2k}}=0,\quad 1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots<i_{2k}\leq\dim\mathfrak{g},

where
where
\operatorname{Pf}
denotes the Pffafian, and
C_{i_{1},\dots,i_{2k}}
is the diagonal submatrix of the skewsymmetric matrix
\mathcal{A}_{x}=\left(c_{ij}^{k}x_{k}\right)
, related to the rows and columns with numbers

i_{1},\dots,i_{2k}
, where
2k=\dim\mathfrak{g}-\operatorname{ind}\mathfrak{g}
.

The subset
\operatorname{Sing}_{0}
is an algebraic variety defined by one homogeneous polynomial equation — the greatest common divisor of the Pfaffians
\operatorname{Pf}C_{i_{1},\dots,i_{2k}}
:

\operatorname{Sing}_{0}=\left\{p_{\mathfrak{g}}(x)=0\right\}.

Definition 2.38
.

p_{\mathfrak{g}}(x)
is called the
fundamental semi-invariant
5
5

5

In some papers, e.g. in
[
22
]
, the term “fundamental semi-invariant” is used for
p_{\mathfrak{g}}(x)^{2}
.
.

We can decompose
p_{\mathfrak{g}}(x)
into a product of irreducible components

p_{\mathfrak{g}}(x)=f_{1}^{s_{1}}(x)\cdot\dots\cdot f_{k}^{s_{k}}(x)

and consider its reduced version

p_{\mathfrak{g},\operatorname{red}}(x)=f_{1}(x)\cdot\dots\cdot f_{k}(x).

(16)

The characteristic numbers can be partitioned into
k

groups
\Lambda_{1},\dots,\Lambda_{k}
according to (
16
). Namely,
\Lambda_{j}
is is the set of roots of
p_{j}(\lambda)=f_{j}(x-\lambda a)
. We reformulate the following statement from
[
5
]
in terms of Jordan tuples. We say that two Jordan tuples
J_{\lambda_{i}}\left(2n_{i1},\dots,2n_{is_{i}}\right)
and
J_{\lambda_{j}}\left(2n_{j1},\dots,2n_{js_{j}}\right)
are
equal
if

s_{i}=s_{j},\qquad\text{ and}\qquad n_{it}=n_{jt},\qquad t=1,\dots,s_{i}.

Proposition 2.39
(A. V. Bolsinov, P. Zhang,
[
5
]
).

1.

The number of distinct characteristic numbers
\lambda_{i}
of
\mathfrak{g}
equals the degree of
p_{\mathfrak{g},\operatorname{red}}(x)
. Similarly, the degree of
p_{\mathfrak{g}}(x)
is the number of characteristic numbers with multiplicities.

2.

More precisely, the number of characteristic numbers in each group
\Lambda_{i}
is equal to the degree
of
f_{i}
.

3.

For characteristic numbers from the same group
\lambda_{j}\in\Lambda_{i}
the number and sizes of Jordan blocks are the same. In other words, all Jordan tuples
J_{\lambda_{j}}(2n_{1},\dots,2n_{s})
for
\lambda_{j}\in\Lambda_{i}
are equal.

4.

The multiplicity
n_{1}+\dots+n_{s}
of a characteristic number
\lambda_{j}\in\Lambda_{i}
is equal to the multiplicity
s_{i}
of

f_{i}
in the decomposition (
16
). In particular, all characteristic numbers within a group
\Lambda_{i}
have
the same multiplicity.

5.

If some of the characteristic numbers have different Jordan tuples, then the set (variety) of
singular elements
\operatorname{Sing}_{0}
is reducible.

Remark 2.40
.

All items in Proposition
2.39
except for Item
3
are from Proposition 5 in
[
5
]
. Item
3
is claimed in
[
5
]
after Proposition 5. It is stated that “it is not hard to show that for characteristic numbers from a fixed group
\Lambda_{i}
, the structure of Jordan blocks is the same too”. Roughly speaking, consider one polynomial
f_{j}(x)
and the corresponding set
S_{j}=\left\{f_{j}(x)=0\right\}
. Consider the lines
y-\lambda a
, where
y\in S_{j}
, and
a\in\mathfrak{g}
. They divide
S_{j}
into a finite number of subsets accoding to the number and sizes of Jordan blocks with eigenvalue
\lambda=0
. One of the subsets contains a non-empty Zariski open subset of
S_{j}\times\mathfrak{g}
. Thus, for a generic line
y-\lambda a
, where
y\in S_{j}
and
a\in\mathfrak{g}
, the pencil
\mathcal{A}_{y}-\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}
has the same number and sizes of Jordan blocks with eigenvalue
\lambda=0
(i.e. “at point
y
”). We call such pairs
(y,a)
“nice”. For a generic line
x-\lambda a
, that intersects
S_{j}
at points
y_{i}=x-\lambda_{i}a,(i=1,\dots,d)
, all the pairs
(y_{i},a)
are “nice”. Note that the lines
x-\lambda a
and
y_{i}-\lambda a
coincide. The Jordan blocks for
\mathcal{A}_{x}-\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
correspond to the Jordan blocks for
\mathcal{A}_{y_{i}}-\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}
with eigenvalue
0
. By definition, all “nice” pairs have the same number and sizes of Jordan blocks with eigenvalue
0
. Thus, all the tuples of Jordan indices
J_{\lambda_{i}}(2n_{1},\dots,2n_{s})
(i.e. “at the points
y_{i}
”) are equal.

2.8
Lie–Poisson pencil

For any finite-dimenstional Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
we have some natural compatible Poisson brackets on its dual space
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
:

•

A linear bracket (
Lie-Poisson bracket
):

\{f,g\}(x):=\langle x,\left[\mathrm{d}f|_{x},\mathrm{d}g|_{x}\right]\rangle.

(17)

•

A constant bracket (so called
“frozen argument bracket”
). Fix any
a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Then the bracket is given by

\{f,g\}_{a}(x):=\langle a,\left[\mathrm{d}f|_{x},\mathrm{d}g|_{x}\right]\rangle.

(18)

We use the canonical isomorphism
(\mathfrak{g}^{*})^{*}=\mathfrak{g}
in (
17
) and (
18
). We also denote the Poisson structures (
17
) and (
18
) as
\mathcal{A}_{x}
and
\mathcal{A}_{a}
. The matrices of these Poisson brackets
\mathcal{A}_{x}=\left(\sum_{i}c^{i}_{jk}x_{i}\right)
and
\mathcal{A}_{a}=\left(\sum_{i}c^{i}_{jk}a_{i}\right)
look similar to the forms (
13
). Yet, there is a difference:

•

In Section
2.7
the elements
x,a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
are fixed. (
13
) defines two skew-symmetric forms.

•

The brackets (
17
) and (
18
) are Poisson brackets on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. The element
a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is fixed.
x\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a variable.

The Lie-Poisson bracket
\mathcal{A}_{x}
is compatible with the constant bracket
\mathcal{A}_{a}
for any
a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. The Poisson pencil

\mathcal{\mathcal{P}}_{a}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}\,\,\bigr|\,\,\lambda\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}\right\}

on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is called a
Lie-Poisson pencil
.

Remark 2.41
.

We can consider JK invariants of a Lie–Poisson pencil
\mathcal{P}_{a}
at each point
x\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
(see to Section
2.6.1
).

•

For any
a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and any
x\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
the Lie–Poisson pencil
\mathcal{P}_{a}
at the point
x
coincides with the pencil
\mathcal{P}_{x,a}
from Section
2.7
.

•

For generic
a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and generic
x\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
the algebraic type of
\mathcal{P}_{x,a}
is given by the JK invariants of
\mathfrak{g}
. This is the algebraic type of the Lie–Poisson pencil
\mathcal{P}_{a}
for generic
a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
.

•

It is possible to study the algebraic type of the Lie–Poisson pencil
\mathcal{P}_{a}
for any
a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Simply speaking, we fix any
a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and take generic
x\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. A similar kind of study is performed in
[
10
]
and
[
11
]
. We do not study the Lie–Poisson pencils
\mathcal{P}_{a}
for arbitrary
a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
in this paper.

3
Kronecker case

Our working horse for realization of JK invariants is the following trivial statement.

Theorem 3.1
.

JK invariants of a sum of Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}
are the union of JK invariants of
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
.

In other words,

\operatorname{JK}\left(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}\right)=\operatorname{JK}\left(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\right)\cup\operatorname{JK}\left(\mathfrak{g}_{2}\right).

(19)

Recall that the multiset
\operatorname{JK}\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
is given by (
14
).

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

For any
x,a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
the pencil
\mathcal{P}_{x,a}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{x+\lambda a}\right\}
takes the form

\mathcal{A}_{x+\lambda a}=\left(\begin{matrix}\mathcal{A}_{x_{1}+\lambda a_{1}}&0\\
0&\mathcal{A}_{x_{2}+\lambda a_{2}}\end{matrix}\right),

where
x_{i},a_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}_{i}
. Thus, the JK decomposition for
\mathcal{A}_{x+\lambda a}
is the sum of the JK decompositions of
\mathcal{A}_{x_{i}+\lambda a_{i}}
. It remains to prove that the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{A}_{x_{i}+\lambda a_{i}}
are different for generic
x,a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Indeed, the eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i}(x_{i},a_{i})
are functions on different Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}_{i}
and by Lemma
4.13
below they are not constants. Thus, the Jordan blocks for
\mathfrak{g}_{i}
have different eigenvalues, and we get the union of multisets (
19
). Theorem
3.1
is proved. ∎

Remark 3.2
.

It can be shown similar to Theorem
3.1
that for a product of Poisson pencils
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
the JK decomposition of
\mathcal{P}_{x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}}
is the sum of JK decompositions of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{x^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}_{x^{\prime}}
.

Example 3.3
.

Assume that

•

JK invariants of
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
are
J_{\lambda_{1}}(2n_{1},2n_{2})
and
2k_{1}-1,2k_{2}-1
,

•

JK invariants of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
are
J_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}(2n^{\prime}_{1})
and
2k^{\prime}_{1}-1
.

Then the JK invariants of
\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}
are

J_{\lambda_{1}}(2n_{1},2n_{2}),\quad J_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}(2n^{\prime}_{1}),\quad 2k_{1}-1,\quad 2k_{2}-1,\quad 2k^{\prime}_{1}-1.

Note that Jordan tuples of
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
always correspond to different eigenvalues in
\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}
.

Thus, realization of JK invariants turns into a “divide and rule” problem. In the Kronecker case we can realize any Kronecker block, therefore all JK invariants are possible.

Theorem 3.4
.

One Kronecker
(2m+1)\times(2m+1)
block,
m\geq 0
, can be realized by the Lie algebra with the basis
g_{1},\dots,g_{m},h_{0},\dots,h_{m}
and the bracket:

[g_{i},h_{0}]=h_{0},\qquad[g_{i},h_{i}]=h_{i},\qquad i=1,\dots,m.

All other commutators are equal to
0
.

In particular, the trivial
1\times 1
Kronecker block is realized by the 1-dimensional abelian Lie algebra. In the corresponding coordinates
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},y_{0},\dots,y_{m})
the matrix of the Lie-Poisson bracket is

\mathcal{A}_{x}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&Y\\
-Y^{T}&0\end{matrix}\right),\qquad Y=\left(\begin{matrix}y_{0}&y_{1}&&\\
\vdots&&\ddots&\\
y_{0}&&&y_{m}\end{matrix}\right).

There is only one Kronecker block, since for a generic pair
(x,a)
we have

\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ker}\left(\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}\right)=1,\qquad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}.

If in the local coordinates
a=(a_{1},\dots,a_{m},b_{0},\dots,b_{m})
, then

\mathrm{Ker}\,\,\left(\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}\right)=\operatorname{span}\left\{\left(\begin{matrix}0_{m}&v\end{matrix}\right)\right\},\quad v=\prod_{j=0}^{m}(y_{j}-\lambda b_{j})\cdot\left(-\frac{1}{y_{0}-\lambda b_{0}},\frac{1}{y_{1}-\lambda b_{1}},\dots,\frac{1}{y_{m}-\lambda b_{m}}\right).

Note that elements of
v
are polynomials in
y,b
, since the numerator
\prod_{j=0}^{m}(y_{j}-\lambda b_{j})
contains all the denumerators
y_{j}-\lambda b_{j}
. The corresponding Casimir function is

f(x,y)=\frac{y_{1}\dots y_{m}}{y_{0}}.

Remark 3.5
.

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
from Theorem
3.4
is solvable. It is a semi-direct sum of abelian lie algebras
\mathbb{C}^{m}\ltimes\mathbb{C}^{m+1}
. Also note that
\operatorname{span}\left\{h_{i}\right\}
are 1-dimensional ideals. In other words,
y_{i}
are linear semi-invariants:

\left\{g(x,y),y_{i}\right\}=\chi_{i}(dg)y_{i}.

We will use that last fact in Section
9
.

Remark 3.6
.

In Theorem
3.4
we could take any semi-direct product
\mathfrak{g}=\mathbb{C}^{m}\ltimes\mathbb{C}^{m+1}
with commutation relations

[g_{i},h_{j}]=\chi_{j}(g_{i})h_{j},\qquad i=1,\dots,m,\quad j=0,\dots,m

where the covectors
\chi_{i}\in\left(\mathbb{C}^{m}\right)^{*}
are in general position. In other words, any
m
of the
m+1
covectors
\chi_{i}
are linearly independant. Similar to Theorem
3.4
, the JK invariants of
\mathfrak{g}
consist of one Kronecker
(2m+1)\times(2m+1)
block.

Remark 3.7
.

In
[
5
]
the Kronecker block is realized by another Lie algebra. Probably, there are many other ways to realize Kronecker blocks. We chose the example that we use below. Note that the JK decomposition of a generic linear pencil
\mathcal{P}=\left\{A+\lambda B\right\}
on an odd-dimensional vector space
V^{2k-1}
consists of one Kronecker
\left(2k-1\right)\times\left(2k-1\right)
block. Thus, one may expect “a generic” odd-dimensional Lie algebra to be Kronecker.

4
Jordan case

In the Jordan case the Lie–Poisson bracket
\mathcal{A}_{x}
is nondegenerate for almost all
x
. That means that
\operatorname{ind}\mathfrak{g}=0
, i.e. in this section we consider Frobenius Lie algebras.

Theorem 4.1
.

Jordan–Kronecker invariants without Kronecker indices can be realized by a Lie algebra if and only if each Jordan tuples
J_{\lambda_{i}}\left(2n_{i1},\dots,2n_{is_{i}}\right)
has a unique maximum, i.e.
n_{i1}>n_{ij}
.

4.1
Realization of invariants

All possible Lie algebras in the Jordan case were unintentionally described by P.Olver in
[
19
]
(we obtained matrices of Lie-Poisson brackets (
20
) and (
21
) below as slight modifications of some linear matrices for symplectic structures in
[
19
]
). By Theorem
3.1
it suffices to realize JK invariants with only one eigenvalue.

•

For one Jordan
2(n+1)\times 2(n+1)
block,
n\geq 0
, the Lie-Poisson bracket is given in coordinates
(p_{0},\dots,p_{n},q_{0},\dots,q_{n})
by

\mathcal{A}_{x}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&P_{n}(p)\\
-P_{n}^{T}(p)&0\end{matrix}\right),\qquad P_{n}(p)=\left(\begin{matrix}p_{0}&&&\\
p_{1}&p_{0}&&\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
p_{n}&\cdots&p_{1}&p_{0}\end{matrix}\right),

(20)

where
p=\left(p_{0},\dots,p_{n}\right)
. For example, for one Jordan
4\times 4
block the Lie–Poisson bracket is

\mathcal{A}_{x}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&0&p_{0}&0\\
0&0&p_{1}&p_{0}\\
-p_{0}&-p_{1}&0&0\\
0&-p_{0}&0&0\end{matrix}\right).

•

Now, how to realize one Jordan tuple with unique maximum. This tuple has the form
J_{\lambda}(2n_{1}+2,2n_{2},\dots,2n_{s})
, where
n_{1}\geq n_{2}\geq\dots\geq n_{s}>0
. The local coordinates are

\left(p_{0},p^{1},\dots,p^{s},q_{0},q^{1},\dots,q^{s}\right),

where
p^{i}
and
q^{i}
denote coordinates
p^{i}_{1},\dots,p^{i}_{n_{i}}
and
q^{i}_{1},\dots,q^{i}_{n_{i}}
respectively. The Lie-Poisson bracket is given by

\mathcal{A}_{x}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&\hat{P}(p)\\
-\hat{P}^{T}(p)&0\end{matrix}\right),\quad\hat{P}(p)=\left(\begin{matrix}p_{0}&&&&\\
p^{1}&P_{n_{1}-1}(\hat{p}^{1})&&&\\
p^{2}&0&P_{n_{2}-1}(\hat{p}^{2})&&\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
p^{n}&0&\cdots&0&P_{n_{s}-1}(\hat{p}^{s})\end{matrix}\right).

(21)

Here
\hat{p}^{i}=\left(p_{0},p^{i}_{1},\dots,p^{i}_{n_{i}-1}\right)
and the submatrices
P_{n_{i}-1}
are as in (
20
). The corresponding Lie bracket also looks nice.

Theorem 4.2
.

One Jordan tuple
J_{\lambda}(2n_{1}+2,2n_{2},\dots,2n_{s})
, where
n_{1}\geq n_{2}\geq\dots\geq n_{s}>0
, can be realized by the Lie algebra with the basis
e_{0},f_{0},e^{i}_{j},f^{i}_{j}
, where
i=1,\dots,s,\,\,j=1,\dots,n_{i}
, and the commutation relations are

[e^{i}_{j},f^{i}_{k}]=e^{i}_{j-k},\qquad j\geq k.

(22)

We formaly assume
e^{i}_{0}=e_{0},f^{i}_{0}=f_{0}
for all
i
.

For the Lie algebra from Theorem
4.2
:

•

the characteristic number

\displaystyle\lambda=\frac{p_{0}(x)}{p_{0}(a)},

•

the singular set

\operatorname{Sing}=\left\{p_{0}=0\right\}.

4.1.1
Proof of Theorem
4.2

First, let us check the Jacobi idenitity for the Lie bracket given by (
22
). The only nontrivial identity is

\left[\left[e^{i}_{j},f^{i}_{k_{1}}\right],f^{i}_{k_{2}}\right]+\left[\left[f^{i}_{k_{1}},f^{i}_{k_{2}}\right],e^{i}_{j}\right]+\left[\left[f^{i}_{k_{2}},e^{i}_{j}\right],f^{i}_{k_{1}}\right]=e_{j-k_{1}-k_{2}}+0-e_{j-k_{1}-k_{2}}=0.

Thus, the Lie algebra form Theorem
4.2
is well-defined. It remains to find the number and types of blocks for
\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}
. This can be easily done using Lemma
2.17
. The next statement is trivial.

Proposition 4.3
.

Consider a pair of
2
-forms on a vector space that have a form

A=\left(\begin{matrix}0&P_{A}\\
-P_{A}^{T}&0\end{matrix}\right),\qquad B=\left(\begin{matrix}0&P_{B}\\
-P_{B}^{T}&0\end{matrix}\right).

Assume that
B
is nondegenerate
\operatorname{Ker}B=0
. The JK invariants of
A
and
B
are determined by the JCF of the matrix
P^{-1}_{B}P_{A}
. More precisely, for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{C},n\in\mathbb{N}
the following numbers of blocks coincide:

•

the number of
2n\times 2n
Jordan blocks with eigenvalue
\lambda
for the pair
A,B
,

•

the number of
n\times n
Jordan blocks with eigenvalue
\lambda
for
P^{-1}_{B}P_{A}
.

Note that in Proposition
4.3
the recursion operator

B^{-1}A=\left(\begin{matrix}\left(P_{B}^{-1}\right)^{T}P_{A}^{T}&0\\
0&P_{B}^{-1}P_{A}\end{matrix}\right)

and the matrices
P_{A}\left(P_{B}^{-1}\right)
and
P_{B}^{-1}P_{A}
are similar. Now, let us consider the case of one Jordan block, i.e.
s=1
. We use the next simple statement.

Proposition 4.4
.

Consider two matrices:

P_{A}=\left(\begin{matrix}a_{1}&&&\\
a_{2}&a_{1}&&\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
a_{n}&\cdots&\cdots&a_{1}\end{matrix}\right),\qquad P_{B}=\left(\begin{matrix}b_{1}&&&\\
b_{2}&b_{1}&&\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
b_{n}&\cdots&\cdots&b_{1}\end{matrix}\right)

If
b_{1}\not=0
and
a_{2}b_{1}\not=a_{1}b_{2}
, then the JCF of
P_{B}^{-1}P_{A}
consists of one Jordan
n\times n
block with eigenvalue
\displaystyle\lambda=\frac{a_{1}}{b_{1}}
.

By Propositions
4.3
and
4.4
for the Lie-Poisson pencil (
20
) and a generic pair
(x,a)
the JK invariants of
\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}
consist of one Jordan
2(n+1)\times 2(n+1)
block. Now consider the general case, where the Lie-Poisson bracket is given by (
21
). Denote

\mathcal{A}_{a}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&\hat{P}(a)\\
-\hat{P}(a)^{T}&0\end{matrix}\right)

By Proposition
4.3
it remains to find the JCF of
\hat{P}(a)^{-1}\hat{P}(p)
. The matrix
\left(\hat{P}(a)^{-1}\hat{P}(p)\right)^{T}
has the same JCF and the following matrices are similar:

\left(\hat{P}(a)^{-1}\hat{P}(p)\right)^{T}\sim\left(\hat{P}(a)^{T}\right)^{-1}\hat{P}(p)^{T}.

Thus, we need to calculate JCF for the matrix
\left(\hat{P}(a)^{T}\right)^{-1}\hat{P}(p)^{T}
. It remains to prove the following statement.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
n_{1}\geq n_{2}\geq\dots\geq n_{s}>0
and let
V
be a vector space with basis
e_{0},e^{i}_{j}
, where
i=1,\dots,s,\,\,j=1,\dots,n_{i}
. For each point
x=(x_{0},x^{1},\dots,x^{s})
, where
x^{i}
denote coordinates
x^{i}_{1},\dots,x^{i}_{n_{i}}
, consider the matrix

M_{x}=\left(\begin{matrix}x_{0}&x^{1}&\dots&\dots&x^{s}\\
&P_{n_{1}-1}^{T}(\hat{x}^{1})&0&\cdots&0\\
&&P_{n_{2}-1}^{T}(\hat{x}^{2})&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&&\ddots&0\\
&&&&P_{n_{s}-1}^{T}(\hat{x}^{s})\end{matrix}\right).

Here
\hat{x}^{i}=\left(x_{0},x^{i}_{1},\dots,x^{i}_{n_{i}-1}\right)
, and the submatrices
P_{n_{i}-1}
are as in (
20
). Then for a generic pair
(x,a)\in V\times V
the JCF of the operator
M_{x}^{-1}M_{a}
consist of Jordan blocks with eigenvalue
\lambda=\frac{x_{0}}{a_{0}}
and sizes

(n_{1}+1)\times(n_{1}+1),\quad n_{2}\times n_{2},\quad\dots,\quad n_{s}\times n_{s}.

Proof of Proposition
4.5
.

Denote
R=M_{x}^{-1}M_{a}
. Obviously, for a generic pair
(x,a)
the operator
R
is nondegenerate. The proof is in several steps.

1.

The operator
R=M_{x}^{-1}M_{a}
has an upper triangular matrix with
\lambda=\frac{x_{0}}{a_{0}}
along the main diagonal. Thus, all Jordan blocks of
M_{x}^{-1}M_{a}
have the eigenvalue
\lambda=\frac{x_{0}}{a_{0}}
.

2.

The vector space
U_{0}=\operatorname{span}(e_{0})
is
R
-invariant and
e_{0}\in\operatorname{Ker}(R-\lambda I)
, since

M_{x}e_{0}=x_{0}e_{0},\qquad M_{a}e_{0}=a_{0}e_{0}.

Next, we use the following simple statement.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
N
be a nilpotent linear operator on
V
and let Jordan blocks of
N
have sizes
k_{1}\geq\dots\geq k_{s}
.

(a)

Any vector
v\in\operatorname{Ker}N
can be extended to a Jordan basis of
N
.

(b)

Extend
v\in\operatorname{Ker}N
to a Jordan basis of
N
. Then
v
lies in a
k_{j}\times k_{j}
Jordan block, where
k_{j}
is the maximal
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
v\in\operatorname{Im}N^{k-1}
.

(c)

The Jordan blocks of
N\bigr|_{V/\operatorname{span}(v)}
have sizes
k_{1},\dots,k_{j-1},k_{j}-1,k_{j+1},\dots,k_{s}
.

3.

The JCF of
R\bigr|_{V/U_{0}}
consists of Jordan blocks with eigenvalue
\lambda
and sizes
n_{1},\dots,n_{s}
. Indeed, the matrices of
M_{x}\bigr|_{V/U_{0}}
are block-diagonal:

\left(\begin{matrix}P_{n_{1}-1}^{T}(\hat{x}^{1})&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&P_{n_{s}-1}^{T}(\hat{x}^{s})\end{matrix}\right).

Hence, we get the sizes of blocks for
R\bigr|_{V/U_{0}}
from Proposition
4.4
.

4.

It remains to prove that
e_{0}\in\operatorname{Im}(R-\lambda I)^{k_{1}}
. The subspace
U_{1}=\operatorname{span}(e_{0},e^{1}_{1},\dots,e^{1}_{n_{1}})
is
R
-invariant and the restriction of the operator has the form

R\bigr|_{U_{1}}=\left(\begin{matrix}\lambda&r_{1}&\dots&r_{n_{1}}\\
&\lambda&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&\ddots&r_{1}\\
&&&\lambda\end{matrix}\right),

where
r_{1}\not=0
for a generic pair
(x,a)
. Hence,
e_{0}\in\operatorname{Im}(R-\lambda I)^{k_{1}}
and by Proposition
4.6
the sizes of Jordan blocks for
R
are
n_{1}+1,n_{2},\dots,n_{s}
, as required.

Proposition
4.5
is proved. ∎

Theorem
4.2
follows from Propositions
4.3
and
4.5
.

4.2
Obstruction in the Jordan case

The obstruction is from differential geometry. Namely, it lies in Turiel’s theorem about the canonical form for compatible symplectic structures, desribed in
[
25
]
. In Sections
4.2.1
-
4.2.4
. we retell main theorems from that article, slightly reformulating them in terms of Poisson pencils. In Section
4.2.5
we use these theorems to prove Theorem
4.1
.

4.2.1
Nondegenerate Poisson pencils

It is well-known that nondegenerate Poisson structures and symplectic structures are equivalent. Let us recall some definitions.

•

Two symplectic forms
(\omega_{0},\omega_{1})
are compatible if and only if the corresponding Poisson brackets
\omega_{0}^{-1}
and
\omega_{1}^{-1}
are compatible.

•

We call a Poisson pencil
\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right\}

nondegenerate
if there is a nondegenerate bracket
\mathcal{A}+\lambda_{0}\mathcal{B},\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}
. Then almost all brackets
\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B},\lambda\in\mathbb{CP}^{1},
are nondegenerate.

•

Let
\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right\}
be a nondegenerate Poisson pencil with nondegenerate bracket
\mathcal{B}
. The
recursion operator
is the field of endomorphisms
P=\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}^{-1}
. For each point
x\in M
the recusion operator defines an operator

P_{x}:T_{x}M\to T_{x}M.

•

We say that the
characteristic polynomial
of the pencil
\mathcal{P}
is the characteristic polynomial of
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}^{-1}
.

•

Two pencils are
isomorpic
if there exists a diffeomorphism that identifies them.

•

The
JK-regular points
for Poisson pencils were defined in Definition
2.30
.

4.2.2
Turiel’s factorization theorem

The next theorem reduces the local description to the case of one eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}
in the complex case (or to the cases of one real eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
or two complex conjugate eigenvalues
\lambda_{1,2}=\alpha\pm i\beta
in the real case).

Theorem 4.7
(F. J. Turiel,
[
25
]
).

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a nondegenerate Poisson pencil on
M
. For any JK-regular point
x\in\left(M,\mathcal{P}\right)
there exists a neighbourhood
Ox
isomorphic to a direct product

(Ox,\mathcal{P})\approx\prod_{i=1}^{n}(O_{i},\mathcal{P}_{i}),

where the characteristic polynomials of
\mathcal{P}_{i}
are irreducible.

Remark 4.8
.

Theorem
4.7
immediately follows from the splitting theorem for Nijenhuis operators (see
[
3
]
). Two symplectic forms
\omega_{0},\omega_{1}
are compatible iff the recursion operator
P=\omega_{0}^{-1}\omega_{1}
is a Nijenhuis operator, i.e. its Nijenhuis tensor vanishes
N_{P}=0
. For more information about Nijenhuis operators see e.g.
[
3
]
.

4.2.3
Flat nondegenerate Poisson pencils

Consider a JK-regular point
x_{0}
of a Poisson pencil with one eigenvalue
\lambda(x)
. In
[
25
]
two cases are discussed:

•

either the eigenvalue
\lambda(x)
is locally constant (
\lambda=\operatorname{const}
)

•

or
x
is a noncritical point (
d\lambda(x)\not=0
).

The first case
\lambda=\operatorname{const}
is trivial.

Definition 4.9
.

A Poisson pencil
\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}\right\}
on
M
is
flat
if for any point
x_{0}\in M
there exist local coordinates
x^{1},\dots,x^{n}
such that all Poisson structures
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
have constant coefficients:

\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}=\sum_{i<j}\left(c_{ij}+\lambda d_{ij}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}},\qquad c_{ij}=\operatorname{const},\quad d_{ij}=\operatorname{const}.

Theorem 4.10
(F. J. Turiel,
[
25
]
).

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a nondegenerate pencil on
M
such that all points
x\in(M,\mathcal{P})
are JK-regular. The pencil
\mathcal{P}
is flat if and only if all its eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}(x)=\operatorname{const}
.

4.2.4
Nondegenerate Poisson pencils with non-constant eigenvalues

Now, we consider the case, when the eigenvalues are noncritical. The next theorem follows from Theorem 3 in
[
25
]
.

Theorem 4.11
(F. J. Turiel,
[
25
]
).

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a nondenerate Poisson pencil on a manifold
M
with one eigenvalue
\lambda(x)
such that
d\lambda(x)\not=0
. Then for a generic JK-regular point the Jordan tuple
J_{\lambda}(2n_{1},\dots,2n_{s})
has a unique maximum, i.e.
n_{1}>n_{j}
.

Remark 4.12
.

Note the term “generic” in Theorem
4.11
. It means that the statement holds on an open dense subset of
M
. Theorem 3 from
[
25
]
is a more precise and general statement than Theorem
4.11
. In particular, in that paper:

1.

“regular” points, where Theorem
4.11
holds, were defined,

2.

canonical coordinates in a neighborhood of a “regular” point were described.

We discuss Theorem
4.11
in more details in Appendix
A
).

4.2.5
Characteristic numbers for Lie-Poisson brackets are nonconstant

In order to apply Theorem
4.11
to the JK invariants of Lie algebras we need the following simple fact.

Lemma 4.13
.

Any eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}(x)
of a Lie-Poisson pencil
\mathcal{A}_{x+\lambda a}
cannot be constant in a neighborhood of any point
x
.

Proof of Lemma
4.13
.

If locally
\lambda_{i}(x)=\operatorname{const}
, then all
x+\lambda a=x+\operatorname{const}
would belong to a singular set
\operatorname{Sing}
for all
x
in some open set. But the singular set
\operatorname{Sing}
is an algebraic variety of the Lie algebra and cannot contain open regions. ∎

Now we can prove Theorem
4.1
.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

By Lemma
4.13
the eigenvalues for Lie-Poisson pencils are noncritical
d\lambda_{i}(x)\not=0
(for a generic point
x
). Thus, by Theorems
4.7
and
4.11
, if the JK invariants have no Kronecker indices, then each Jordan tuple has a unique maximum. Any collection of Jordan tuples such that each tuple has a unique maximum was realized in Section
4.1
. Theorem
4.1
is proved. ∎

5
Core and mantle distributions

Before discussing the mixed case for the JK invariants let us introduce several notions and prove several facts about compatible Poisson brackets
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
on a manifold
M
. Namely, in this section we

•

introduce the core distribution
\mathcal{K}
and the mantle distribution
\mathcal{M}
(Definition
5.1
),

•

prove their integrability (Propositions
5.3
and
5.8
),

•

and introduce some useful local coordinates (Theorems
5.9
and
5.12
).

5.1
Core and mantle distributions

In this section we discuss distributions on bi-Poisson manifolds
(M,\mathcal{P})
. For the terminology and more details about singular distributions and their integrability see
[
7
]
. For any distribution
\Delta\subset TM
we can also consider its dual distribution
\Delta^{0}\subset T^{*}M
. A distribution
\Delta^{0}
in the cotangent bundle
T^{*}M
will be called integrable (involutive, etc) if
\Delta
is integrable (respectively, involutive, etc).

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}=\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right\}
be a Poisson pencil on
M
. There are two natural distributions in the cotangent bundle
T^{*}M
:

•

the
core distribution

\mathcal{K}=\bigoplus_{\lambda-\text{regular}}\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{A}_{\lambda},

(23)

•

the
mantle distribution

\mathcal{M}=\left(\bigoplus_{\lambda-\text{regular}}\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}\right)^{\displaystyle\perp}.

One can also consider the dual distributions
\mathcal{K}^{0}
and
\mathcal{M}^{0}
in the tangent bundle
TM
. We call
\mathcal{K}^{0}
and
\mathcal{M}^{0}
the
dual-core
and the
dual-mantle
distributions, respectively. In
[
26
]

\mathcal{K}^{0}
and
\mathcal{M}^{0}
are called a
primary
and a
secondary axis
, respectively. Note that

\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{M}\subset T^{*}M\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\mathcal{M}^{0}\subset\mathcal{K}^{0}\subset TM.

So far,
\mathcal{K},\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{K}^{0},\mathcal{M}^{0}
are singular distribution, i.e. to each point
x\in M
we assign a vector subspace of
T^{*}M
or
TM
. We denote that subspaces as
\mathcal{K}_{x},\mathcal{M}_{x}
, etc. Propositions
5.3
and
5.8
show that they are integrable regular distributions in a neighborhood of a generic point. First, we give another description for
\mathcal{K}^{0}
and
\mathcal{M}^{0}
that easily follows from the JK theorem.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}=\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right\}
be a Poisson pencil on
M
. Then for any point
x\in M
we have the following.

1.

The dual-core distribution is

\mathcal{K}^{0}=\bigcap_{\lambda-\text{regular}}\mathrm{Im}\,\,\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}.

(24)

2.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
be a regular bracket at
x
and let
\omega_{\lambda}
be the corresponding symplectic form on
\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
. Then the dual-mantle distribution is

\mathcal{M}^{0}=\operatorname{Ker}\left(\omega_{\lambda}\bigr|_{\mathcal{K}^{0}}\right).

5.1.1
Integrability of the core distribution

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}=\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right\}
be a Poisson pencil on a manifold
M
and
p_{\mathcal{P}}(x)
be its characteristic polynomial at
x\in M
.

1.

If
\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}(x)=\operatorname{const}
in a neighbourhood
Ox_{0}\subset(M,\mathcal{P})
, then the dual-core distribution
\mathcal{K}^{0}
is an integrable singular smooth distribution in
Ox_{0}
.

2.

For any point
x\in M
we have

\begin{gathered}\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{K}_{x}=\dim M-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}(x)-\deg p_{\mathcal{P}}(x),\\
\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{K}^{0}_{x}=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}(x)+\deg p_{\mathcal{P}}(x).\end{gathered}

(25)

Thus, if
