\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}(x)=\operatorname{const}
and
\deg p_{\mathcal{P}}(x)=\operatorname{const}
in a neighbourhood
Ox_{0}\subset(M,\mathcal{P})
, then the dual-core distribution
\mathcal{K}^{0}
is an integrable regular distribution in
Ox_{0}
.

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

For any Poisson pencil
\mathcal{A}
the characteristic distribution
\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{A}
is an integrable singular smooth distribution. If
\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{A}=\operatorname{const}
in a neighbourhood of
x
, then locally
\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{A}
is an integrable regular distribution. By Proposition
2.29
and (
24
) the dual-core distribution
\mathcal{K}^{0}
is an intersection of a finite number of such distributions
\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{A}_{\lambda_{i}}
. Formula (
25
) for the dimension of the core
\mathcal{K}
follows from Proposition
2.19
. Proposition
5.3
is proved. ∎

Remark 5.4
.

The intersection of all characteristic distributions
\displaystyle\bigcap_{\lambda\in\operatorname{KP}^{1}}\mathrm{Im}\,\,\left(\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right)
is an integrable singular smooth distribution at any point
x\in M
. This is another natural distribution, we don’t study it in this paper. We also do not discuss singularities of the core or the mantle distributions.

5.1.2
Caratheodory–Jacobi–Lie theorem for Poisson manifolds

Next, we want to prove the integrability of the mantle distribution
\mathcal{M}
(at a generic point). To do it we will need the following variant of Caratheodory–Jacobi–Lie theorem for Poisson manifolds. It is a slight modification of Theorem 2.1 from
[
18
]
.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
(M,\mathcal{A})
be a Poisson manifold,
\dim M=n
and
\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{A}=2k
on
M
. Assume that

•

z_{1},\dots,z_{n-2k}
are Casimir functions, i.e.
\left\{f,z_{j}\right\}=0
,

•

p_{1},\dots,p_{r}
, where
r\leq k
, are smooth functions in involution
\left\{p_{i},p_{j}\right\}=0
,

•

dp_{1},\dots,dp_{r}
and
dz_{1},\dots,dz_{n-2k}
are linearly independent at
x\in M
, i.e.

\left(dp_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge dp_{r}\wedge dz_{1}\wedge\dots dz_{n-2k}\right)\bigr|_{x}\not=0.

Then there exist functions
p_{r+1},\dots,p_{k},q_{1},\dots,q_{k}
such that
(p_{i},q_{i},z_{j})
are local coordinates at
x
and

\mathcal{A}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{i}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{i}}.

(26)

Proof of Theorem
5.5
.

Since
dp_{1},\dots,dp_{r},dz_{1},\dots,dz_{n-2k}
are linearly independant and

\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{A}=\operatorname{span}\left\{dz_{1},\dots,dz_{n-2k}\right\}

the Hamiltonian vector fields
X_{p_{1}},\dots,X_{p_{r}}
are linearly independant. By Theorem 2.1 from
[
18
]
there exists local coordinates
p_{1},\dots,p_{r},q_{1},\dots,q_{r},s_{1},\dots,s_{n-2r}
such that

\mathcal{A}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{i}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{i}}+\sum_{i,j=1}^{n-2r}g_{ij}(s)\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{i}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{j}}.

It remains to note that
z_{j}
are Casimir functions for the Poisson bivector

\sum_{i,j=1}^{n-2r}g_{ij}(s)\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{i}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{j}}

and apply the Darboux-Weinstein theorem
2.25
for it. Theorem
5.5
is proved. ∎

We need Theorem
5.5
for the following statement. Recall that a distribution in the cotangent bundle
\Delta\subset T^{*}M
is integrable if and only if its dual distribution
\Delta^{0}\subset TM
is integrable.

Corollary 5.6
.

Let
(M,\mathcal{A})
be a Poisson manifold and
\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{A}=2k
on
M
. Let
\Delta\subset T^{*}M
be a regular isotropic distribution such that
\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{A}\subset\Delta
. If
\Delta
is integrable, then
\Delta^{\perp}
is also integrable.

Proof of Corollary
5.6
.

Take any point
x\in M
.

•

Let
z_{1},\dots,z_{n-2k}
be local Casimir functions at
x
, i.e.
\left\{f,z_{j}\right\}=0
,.

•

Since
\Delta
is integrable and
\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{A}\subset\Delta
, there exists functions
p_{1},\dots,p_{r}
such that
dp_{1},\dots,dp_{r}
and
dz_{1},\dots,dz_{n-2k}
are linearly independent at
x\in M
, i.e.

\left(dp_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge dp_{r}\wedge dz_{1}\wedge\dots dz_{n-2k}\right)\bigr|_{x}\not=0,

and
\Delta
is locally given by the level sets of the functions
p_{i},z_{j}
, i.e.

\Delta=\operatorname{span}\left\{dp_{1},\dots,dp_{r},dz_{1},\dots,dz_{n-2k}\right\}.

•

Since
\Delta
is isotropic, the functions
p_{1},\dots,p_{r}
are in involution
\left\{p_{i},p_{j}\right\}=0
.

Thus, we can apply Theorem
5.5
and get local coordinates

p_{1},\dots,p_{k},q_{1},\dots,q_{k},z_{1},\dots,z_{n-2k}

such that (
26
) holds. In this coordinates

\Delta^{\perp}=\operatorname{span}\left\{dp_{1},\dots,dp_{k},dq_{r+1},\dots,dq_{k},dz_{1},\dots,dz_{n-2k}\right\}.

Therefore,
\Delta^{\perp}
is integrable. Corollary
5.6
is proved. ∎

Remark 5.7
.

In the holomorhpic case the proof remains the same, but one should use holomorphic analogues of some theorems. For instance, instead of the Frobenious theorem one can use the fact that involutive holomorphic subbundles are integrable in the holomorphic sense (see e.g.
[
27
]
).

5.1.3
Integrability of the mantle distribution.

Applying Corollary
5.6
to the core distribution
\mathcal{K}
we get the following.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}=\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right\}
be a Poisson pencil on
M
and
p_{\mathcal{P}}(x)
be its characteristic polynomial at
x\in M
.

1.

For any point
x\in M
we have

\begin{gathered}\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{M}_{x}=\dim M-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}(x)+\deg p_{\mathcal{P}}(x),\\
\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{M}^{0}_{x}=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}(x)-\deg p_{\mathcal{P}}(x).\end{gathered}

(27)

2.

Thus, if
\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}(x)=\operatorname{const}
and
\deg p_{\mathcal{P}}(x)=\operatorname{const}
in a neighbourhood
Ox_{0}\subset(M,\mathcal{P})
, then the dual-mantle distribution
\mathcal{M}^{0}
is an integrable regular distribution in
Ox_{0}
.

Note that formula (
27
) for the dimension of the core
\mathcal{M}
follows from Proposition
2.19
.

5.2
Local coordinates for core and mantle

Theorem 5.9
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}=\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right\}
be a Poisson pencil on
M
and
p_{\mathcal{P}}(x)
be its characteristic polynomial at
x\in M
. Assume that

\deg p_{\mathcal{P}}(x)=\operatorname{const},\qquad\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}(x)=\operatorname{const}

on
M
. Denote

n_{J}=\dim p_{\mathcal{P}}(x_{0}),\qquad m=\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}(x_{0})-2n_{J},\qquad r=\operatorname{corank}\mathcal{P}.

Then for any point
x\in M
there exist local coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{m},s_{1},\dots,s_{2n_{J}},y_{1},\dots,y_{m+r}
such that the core and mantle distribution are

\mathcal{K}=\operatorname{span}\left\{dy_{1},\dots,dy_{m+r}\right\},\qquad\mathcal{M}=\operatorname{span}\left\{ds_{1},\dots,ds_{2n_{J}},dy_{1},\dots,dy_{m+r}\right\}

(28)

and the pencil has the form

\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\wedge v_{\lambda,i}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 2n_{J}}c_{\lambda,ij}(s,y)\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{i}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{j}}

for some vector fields
v_{\lambda,i}=v_{\lambda,i}(x,s,y)
and some functions
c_{\lambda,ij}(s,y)
.

Simply speaking, in the coordinatex
(x,s,y)
from Theorem
5.9
the matrices of the Poisson brackets take the form

\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}=\left(\begin{matrix}*&*&*\\
*&C_{\lambda}(s,y)&0\\
*&0&0\end{matrix}\right),

(29)

where
*
are some matrices. Obviously, the vector fields
v_{\lambda,i}=v_{\lambda,i}(x,s,y)
and the functions
c_{\lambda,ij}(s,y)
depend linearly on
\lambda
:

v_{\lambda,i}=v_{0,i}+\lambda v_{\infty,i},\qquad c_{\lambda,ij}(s,y)=c_{0,ij}(s,y)+\lambda c_{\infty,ij}(s,y).

Remark 5.10
.

Slightly informaly, in the coordinates
(x,s,y)
from Theorem
5.9
the core and the mantle distributions are

\mathcal{K}=\operatorname{span}\left\{dy\right\},\qquad\mathcal{M}=\operatorname{span}\left\{ds,dy\right\}.

The dual-core and dual-mantle distributions have the form

\mathcal{K}^{0}=\operatorname{span}\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial x},\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\right\},\qquad\mathcal{M}^{0}=\operatorname{span}\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\right\}.

Proof of Theorem
5.9
.

The proof is in several steps.

1.

By Propositions
5.8
and
5.3
the core
\mathcal{K}
and mantle
\mathcal{M}
are regular integrable distributions and
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{M}
. Thus, there exist local coordinates such that
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{M}
have the form (
28
).

2.

Recall that the core
\mathcal{K}
is bi-isotropic
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}\bigr|_{\mathcal{K}}=0
(see Corollary
2.7
), thus

\left\{y_{i},y_{j}\right\}_{\lambda}=0.

3.

By definition,
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
and hence

\left\{s_{i},y_{j}\right\}_{\lambda}=0.

After that step the matrices of Poisson bracktes have the form

\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}=\left(\begin{matrix}*&*&*\\
*&C_{\lambda}(x,s,y)&0\\
*&0&0\end{matrix}\right).

4.

It remains to prove that
c_{\lambda,ij}=\left\{s_{i},s_{j}\right\}_{\lambda}
do not depend on
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}
. It follows from the Jacobi identity:

\left\{y_{k},\left\{s_{i},s_{j}\right\}_{\lambda}\right\}_{\lambda}=\left\{\left\{y_{k},s_{i},\right\}_{\lambda}s_{j}\right\}_{\lambda}+\left\{s_{i},\left\{y_{k},s_{j}\right\}_{\lambda}\right\}_{\lambda}=0.

Consider the Hamiltonian vector fields

\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}dy_{k}=\left\{y_{k},\cdot\right\}_{\lambda}.

Using the JK theorem, it is easy to check that, since
dy_{1},\dots,dy_{m+r}
is a basis of the core
\mathcal{K}
,

\operatorname{span}\left\{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}dy_{1},\dots,\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}dy_{m+r}\right\}=\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{m}}\right\}

for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{KP}^{1}
. We get that

\frac{\partial\left\{s_{i},s_{j}\right\}_{\lambda}}{\partial x_{k}}=0,\qquad k=1,\dots,m,\qquad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{KP}^{1}

and thus
c_{\lambda,ij}=c_{\lambda,ij}(s,y)
, as required.

Theorem
5.9
is proved. ∎

5.2.1
Eigenvalue decomposition

We can slightly generalize Theorem
5.9
and reduce some problems about Poisson pencils
\mathcal{P}
to the case when

•

there is only one eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}
,

•

and all Kronecker blocks of
\mathcal{P}
are trivial
1\times 1
blocks.

Let
(x,s,y)
be the coordinates from Theorem
5.9
. Rouhly speaking, coordinates
s
correspond to the sum of Jordan blocks
V_{J}
. Recall that the Jordan blocks can be grouped by eigenvalues:

V_{J}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{p}V_{J_{\lambda_{i}}},\qquad V_{J_{\lambda_{i}}}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{s_{i}}V_{J_{\lambda_{i},2n_{ij}}}.

Using Turiel’s factorization theorem
4.7
we can “group” the coordinates
s
by eigenvalues.

Remark 5.11
.

We formulate the next theorem for Poisson pencils with trivial
1\times 1
Kronecker blocks. For a general pencil
\mathcal{P}
, we can apply that theorem for the pencil
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{red}}
, given by (
114
). Simpy speaking, we can use it for the subset
(s,y)
of the coordinates from Theorem
5.9
.

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right\}
be a Poisson pencil on a manifold
M
and let
x_{0}\in(M,\mathcal{P})
be a JK-regular point. Assume that locally the Kronecker blocks of
\mathcal{P}
are
r
trivial
1\times 1
Kronecker blocks. Then in a neighbourhood of
x_{0}
there exists local coordinates

(s^{1}_{1},\dots,s^{1}_{2n_{1}},\dots,s^{p}_{1},\dots,s^{p}_{2n_{p}},z_{1},\dots,z_{r})

(30)

such that the matrices of Poisson brackets have the form

\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}=\left(\begin{matrix}C^{1}_{\lambda}(s^{1},z)&&&\\
&\ddots&&\\
&&C^{p}_{\lambda}(s^{p},z)&\\
&&&0_{r}\end{matrix}\right)

(31)

and the characterictic polynomials
p_{t}(\lambda)
of the pencils
\left\{C^{t}_{\lambda}(s^{p},z)\right\}
are irreducible.

In other words, the pencil in Theorem
5.12
has the form

\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}=\sum_{t=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 2n_{t}}c^{t}_{\lambda,ij}(s^{t},z)\frac{\partial}{\partial s^{t}_{i}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial s^{t}_{j}}\right)

for some functions
c^{t}_{\lambda,ij}(s^{t}_{1},\dots,s^{t}_{2n_{t}},z_{1},\dots,z_{r})
.

Proof of Theorem
5.12
.

Since all Kronecker blocks are
1\times 1
all regular forms
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
have common (local) Casimir functions
z_{1},\dots,z_{r}
. They also have the same symplectic leaves
(S_{z},\omega_{\lambda,z})
, i.e. level sets of Casimir functions:

S_{z}=\left\{z_{1}=\operatorname{const},\dots,z_{r}=\operatorname{const}\right\}.

We can decompose each symplectic
(S_{z},\omega_{\lambda,z})
using Turiel’s factorization theorem
4.7
. We get coordinates (
30
) such that the matrices of all forms
\omega_{\lambda,z}
are block-diagonal:

\omega_{\lambda,z}=\left(\begin{matrix}\Omega^{1}_{\lambda}(s^{1},z)&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&\Omega^{p}_{\lambda}(s^{p},z)\end{matrix}\right).

Since
z_{i}
are Casimir function, the pencil
\mathcal{P}
takes the form (
31
). Theorem
5.12
is proved. ∎

Corollary 5.13
.

In the coordinates from Theorem
5.12
the eigenvalue
\lambda_{t}
, corresponding to the block
C^{t}_{\lambda}(s^{t},z)
, is a function of
s^{t}
and
z
, i.e.
\lambda_{t}=\lambda_{t}(s^{t},z)
.

Note that the coordinates
s^{t}
correspond to the sum of Jordan blocks with eigenvalue
\lambda_{t}
. Thus, for a generic Poisson pencil we can reformulate Corollary
5.13
as follows.

Corollary 5.14
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a Poisson pencil on
M
that in a neighbouhood of
x\in M
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
5.9
and has characteristic numbers
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{p}
. Denote by
\mathcal{J}_{\lambda_{i}}
the sum of all Jordan blocks with eigenvlaue
\lambda_{i}
(it is a distribution in
T^{*}M
). Then

d\lambda_{i}\in\mathcal{J}_{\lambda_{i}}\oplus\mathcal{K}.

The distributions
\mathcal{J}_{\lambda_{i}}\oplus\mathcal{K}
are pairwise orthogonal w.r.t. all brackets
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}
. Therefore, the characteristic numbers are in involutions w.r.t. all brackets
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
:

\left\{\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}\right\}_{\lambda}=0.

Below, we prove a more general statement in Lemma
9.8
.

6
Mixed case, trivial Kronecker blocks

One of the simplest examples of Lie algebras with mixed JK invariants is the Heisenberg algebra with the basis
e,f,h
and relation
[e,f]=h
. The Lie-Poisson has the form

\mathcal{A}_{x}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&z&0\\
-z&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{matrix}\right),

and the eigenvalue is
\displaystyle\lambda(x,a)=\frac{\langle x,h\rangle}{\langle a,h\rangle}
.

All possible JK invariants with trivial Kroncker blocks can be realized similar to the Heizenberg algebra. Roughly speaking, one Kronecker block can contain only one eigenvalue of a Jordan tuple with multiple maxima. There are no other limitations.

Theorem 6.1
.

The following Jordan–Kronecker invariants with only trivial
1\times 1
Kronecker blocks can be realized by Lie algebras:

1.

For
1
trivial Kronecker block there is no more than
1
Jordan tuple with multiple maxima
J_{\lambda_{i}}(2n_{i1},\dots,2n_{is_{i}})
,
n_{i1}=n_{i2}\geq n_{ij}
.

2.

For several trivial Kronecker blocks any collection of Jordan tuples is possible.

In Section
6.1.2
we realize several Jordan tuples with multiple maxima and one
1\times 1
Kronecker block by a pencil
\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}
, where the Poisson bracket
\mathcal{A}
is affine (i.e. linear + constant) and
\mathcal{B}
is constant.

6.1
Realization

1.

First, let us realize one Jordan tuple
J_{\lambda}(2n_{1},\dots,2n_{s})
with one trivial Kronecker block. The formulas are similar to (
21
). The local coordinates are

\left(p^{1},\dots,p^{s},q^{1},\dots,q^{s},z\right),

where
p^{i}
and
q^{i}
denote coordinates
p^{i}_{1},\dots,p^{i}_{n_{i}}
and
q^{i}_{1},\dots,q^{i}_{n_{i}}
respectively. The Lie-Poisson bracket is given by

\mathcal{A}_{x}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&P_{J}(z)&0\\
-P_{J}^{T}(z)&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{matrix}\right),

(32)

where

P_{J}(z)=\left(\begin{matrix}P_{n_{1}}(z)&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&P_{n_{s}}(z)\end{matrix}\right),\qquad P_{n_{i}}(z)=\left(\begin{matrix}z&&&\\
p_{1}&z&&\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
p_{n_{i}-1}&\cdots&p_{1}&z\end{matrix}\right).

The commutation relations are similar to (
22
). In a basis
e^{i}_{j},f^{i}_{j},h
, where
i=1,\dots,s,\,\,j=1,\dots,n_{i}
they are

[e^{i}_{j},f^{i}_{k}]=\begin{cases}e^{i}_{j-k},\qquad j>k,\\
h,\qquad j=k.\end{cases}

(33)

The eigenvalue
\displaystyle\lambda=\frac{z(x)}{z(a)}
. The singular set
\operatorname{Sing}
is given by
z=0
.

2.

By Theorem
3.1
it remains to realize several Jordan tuples

J_{\lambda_{i}}(2n_{i1},\dots,2n_{is_{i}}),\qquad i=1,\dots,p

with two trivial Kronecker blocks. The realization is similar to (
32
). We take a two-dimensional center with coordinates
z_{1},z_{2}
and add Jordan tuples as in (
32
), replacing
z
with different linear combinations of
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
:

\mathcal{A}_{x}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&P_{J_{1}}(z_{1}+z_{2})&&&&&\\
-P_{J_{1}}^{T}(z_{1}+z_{2})&0&&&&&\\
&&\ddots&&&\\
&&&0&P_{J_{p}}(z_{1}+pz_{2})&&\\
&&&-P_{J_{p}}^{T}(z_{1}+pz_{2})&0&\\
&&&&&0&0\\
&&&&&0&0\end{matrix}\right).

Theorem 6.2
.

Two Kronecker
1\times 1
blocks and
q
Jordan tuples
J_{\lambda_{t}}(2n_{t1},\dots,2n_{ts_{i}})
,
t=1,\dots,p
, can be realized by the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
with the basis
e^{i}_{tj},f^{i}_{tj},h_{1},h_{2}
, where
t=1,\dots,p,i=1,\dots,s_{i},\,\,j=1,\dots,n_{ti}
, and the commutation relations are

[e^{i}_{tj},f^{i}_{tk}]=\begin{cases}e^{i}_{t,j-k},\qquad j>k,\\
h_{1}+th_{2},\qquad j=k.\end{cases}

(34)

The eigenvalues are

\lambda_{j}=\frac{z_{1}(x)+jz_{2}(x)}{z_{1}(a)+jz_{2}(a)},\qquad j=1,\dots,p.

The singular set
\operatorname{Sing}
is given by

\prod_{j=1}^{p}(z_{1}+jz_{2})=0.

Proof of Theorem
6.2
.

It is easy to check the Jacobi identity, we prove a slightly more general statement in Proposition
6.3
below. Obviously, the elements
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
belong to the center
Z(\mathfrak{g})
and correspond to the trivial
1\times 1
Kronecker blocks. Denote
U=\operatorname{span}(e^{i}_{tj},f^{i}_{tj})
. It follows from Propositions
4.3
and
4.4
that the JK invariants for
\left(\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}\right)\bigr|_{U}
are
q
Jordan tuples
J_{\lambda_{t}}(2n_{t1},\dots,2n_{ts_{i}})
. Thus, the JK invariants for
\mathfrak{g}
are these Jordan tuples and two trivial Kronecker blocks. Theorem
6.2
is proved. ∎

6.1.1
Proof of Jacobi identity

Let us check the Jacobi identity for these Lie algebras. We consider a slightly more general Poisson bracket, with nonlinear characteristic numbers, and prove the Jacobi identity for it.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
(p^{i}_{tj},q^{i}_{tj},z_{1},\dots,z_{d})
, where
t=1,\dots,p,i=1,\dots,s_{t},\,\,j=1,\dots,n_{ti}
be linear coordinates on a vector space. For any constants
c^{i}_{tj}
and functions
f_{t}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})
the Poisson bracket given by

\left\{p^{i}_{tj},q^{i}_{tk}\right\}=\begin{cases}p^{i}_{t,j-k}+c^{i}_{t,j-k},\qquad&j>k,\\
f_{t}(z),\qquad&j=k.\end{cases}

(35)

is well-defined. The brackets between other coordinate functions are
0
.

In other words, the matrix of the bracket (
35
) has the form

\mathcal{A}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&P_{1,1}&&&&\\
-P_{1,1}^{T}&0&&&&\\
&&\ddots&&&\\
&&&0&P_{p,s_{p}}&\\
&&&-P_{p,s_{p}}^{T}&0&\\
&&&&&0_{d}\end{matrix}\right),

where
0_{d}
is the
d\times d
zero matrix and

P_{t,i}=\left(\begin{matrix}f_{t}(z)&&&\\
p^{i}_{t1}+c^{i}_{t1}&f_{t}(z)&&\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
p^{i}_{t,n_{ti}-1}+c^{i}_{t,n_{ti}-1}&\cdots&p^{i}_{t,n_{1}}+c^{i}_{t1}&f_{t}(z)\end{matrix}\right).

Obviously, the Lie-Poisson brackets given by (
33
) and (
34
) have the form (
35
).

Proof of Proposition
6.3
.

The Jacobi identity is proved by direct calculation. Note that any bracket
\left\{f,g\right\}
is a function of
p
and
z
. Also
\left\{f,z_{j}\right\}=0
for any function
f
. Thus, the only nontrivial Jacobi identity is for the coordinate functions
p^{i}_{tj},q^{i}_{tk_{1}},q^{i}_{tk_{2}}
. Formaly denote

p^{i}_{t0}=f_{i}(z),\qquad p^{i}_{tk}=c^{i}_{tk}=c^{i}_{t0}=0,\quad k<0.

Note that
\left\{f,p^{i}_{t0}\right\}=0
for any function
f
and that the formula (
35
) formally holds for
p^{i}_{tj},j\leq 0
. We get the required Jacobi identity:

\displaystyle\left\{p^{i}_{tj}\left\{q^{i}_{tk_{1}},q^{i}_{tk_{2}}\right\}\right\}+\left\{q^{i}_{tk_{1}}\left\{q^{i}_{tk_{2}},p^{i}_{tj}\right\}\right\}+\left\{q^{i}_{tk_{2}}\left\{p^{i}_{tj},q^{i}_{tk_{1}}\right\}\right\}=

\displaystyle=0+(p^{i}_{t,j-k_{1}-k_{2}}+c^{i}_{t,j-k_{1}-k_{2}})-(p^{i}_{t,j-k_{1}-k_{2}}+c^{i}_{t,j-k_{1}-k_{2}})=0.

Proposition
6.3
is proved. ∎

6.1.2
Realization of JK invariants with one trivial Kronecker block by Poisson pencils

We cannot realize several Jordan tuples with multiple maxima and one trivial
1\times 1
Kronecker block by a linear Lie-Poisson bracket
\mathcal{A}_{x}
and a constant bracket
\mathcal{A}_{a}
. We can easily realize these JK invariants, if we loosen the conditions a little bit, and consider a pair of compatible brackets
\mathcal{A}+\lambda{B}
, where
\mathcal{A}
is affine (i.e. linear + constant) and
\mathcal{B}
is constant. The idea is simple, we take the consider the brackets from the Section
6.1.1
of the form

\mathcal{A}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&P_{J_{1}}(f_{1}(z))&&&&\\
-P_{J_{1}}^{T}(f_{1}(z))&0&&&&\\
&&\ddots&&&\\
&&&0&P_{J_{p}}(f_{p}(z))&&\\
&&&-P_{J_{p}}^{T}(f_{p}(z))&0&\\
&&&&&0_{d}\end{matrix}\right).

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
(p^{i}_{tj},q^{i}_{tj},z_{1},\dots,z_{d})
, where
t=1,\dots,p,i=1,\dots,s_{i},\,\,j=1,\dots,n_{ti}
be linear coordinates on a vector space
V
. Let
d_{t}\not=0
and
c^{i}_{tj}
be constants,
f_{t}(z)
be smooth functions. Consider a pair of compatible Poisson bracket
\left\{\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right\}
given by

\left\{p^{i}_{tj},q^{i}_{tk}\right\}_{\lambda}=\begin{cases}p^{i}_{t,j-k}+\lambda c^{i}_{j-k},\qquad&j>k,\\
f_{t}(z)+\lambda d_{t},\qquad&j=k.\end{cases}

(36)

For a generic point
x\in V
the JK invariants of
\mathcal{A}\bigr|_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\bigr|_{x}
are
d
trivial
1\times 1
Kronecker blocks and
q
Jordan tuples
J_{\lambda_{t}}(2n_{t1},\dots,2n_{ts_{i}})
with eigenvalue
\displaystyle\lambda_{t}=\frac{f_{t}(z)}{d_{t}}
.

Remark 6.5
.

If some eigenvalues
\lambda_{t}=\frac{f_{t}(z)}{d_{t}}
in Theorem
6.4
are equal, then Jordan tuples with the same eigenvalues should be unified.

Remark 6.6
.

Since we can choise various functions
f_{t}(z)
in Theorem
6.4
, we can take
d=1
and
f_{t}(z)=z+t
. We get a realization of one
1\times 1
Kronecker block and any collection of Jordan tuples by compatible Poisson brackets. The coefficients of the bracket
\mathcal{A}
are affine functions, and
\mathcal{B}
is a constant bracket.

Proof of Theorem
6.4
.

By Proposition
6.3
for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
the Poisson bracket
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}=\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}
given by (
36
) is well-defined. That means that the Poisson brackets
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are compatible. The calculation of JK invariant is similar to the proof of Theorem
6.2
. Theorem
6.4
is proved. ∎

6.2
Obstruction

We need to prove the following.

Theorem 6.7
.

There are no Lie algebras with only one Kronecker
1\times 1
block and more than one Jordan tuple with multiple maxima.

The proof of Theorem
6.7
is in 2 steps:

1.

For any Jordan tuple
J_{\lambda}(2n_{1},\dots,2n_{s})
with multiple maxima the differential of its eigenvalue
d\lambda
lies in the the core
\mathcal{K}
(Lemma
6.8
). Here
\mathcal{K}
coincides with the
1\times 1
Kronecker block.

2.

One
1\times 1
Kronecker block is “not big enough” for two eigenvalues of a Lie-Poisson pencil (Lemma
6.12
).

Once again, as for Theorem
4.1
, the obstruction is a combination of a fact from differential geometry and a fact about Lie algebras.

6.2.1
Eigenvalues of Jordan tuples with multiple maxima

We want to prove the following.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right\}
be a Poisson pencil on a manifold
M
and
x_{0}\in(M,\mathcal{P})
be a JK-regular point. If the Jordan tuple
J_{\lambda_{0}}(2n_{1},\dots,2n_{s})
of an eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}
has multiple maxima (i.e.
n_{1}=n_{2}\geq n_{j}
), then

d\lambda_{0}\in\bigoplus_{\lambda-\text{regular}}\operatorname{Ker}\left(\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right)=\mathcal{K}.

(37)

We use the next statement, which is a special case of Turiel’s decomposition theorem. Namely, it is a particular case of
[
26
, Theorem 7.1]
for trivial Kronecker blocks and one eigenvalue.

Theorem 6.9
(F. J. Turiel,
[
26
]
).

Let
\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right\}
be a Poisson pencil on a manifold
M
such that all points
x\in M
are JK-regular and the JK invariants are

•

r
trivial
1\times 1
Kronecker blocks,

•

one Jordan tuple
J_{\lambda_{0}}(2n_{1},\dots,2n_{s})
.

Also assume that either

\lambda_{0}(x)\equiv\operatorname{const},\qquad\text{ or }\qquad d\lambda_{0}(x_{0})\not\in\mathcal{K}.

Here the core
\mathcal{K}
is the sum of
1\times 1
Kronecker blocks. Then in a neighbourhood
Ox_{0}
of a generic point
x_{0}\in M
the pencil
\mathcal{P}
decomposes into a product of a Jordan pencil
\mathcal{P}_{Jord}
and a trivial Kronecker pencil:

(Ox_{0},\mathcal{P})\approx\left(M_{\textrm{Jord}},\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{Jord}}\right)\times\left(M_{\textrm{Kron}},0\right).

Remark 6.10
.

Note that Theorem
6.9
holds for a generic point
x_{0}\in M
, i.e. it holds on an open dense subset of
M
. We discuss Turiel’s decomposition theorem in Appendix
B
.

Theorem
6.9
states that for a generic point
x_{0}\in M
there exist local coordinates
s_{1},\dots,s_{2n},z_{1},\dots,z_{r}
such that the matrices of
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
take the form

\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}=\left(\begin{matrix}C_{\lambda}(s)&0\\
0&0\end{matrix}\right).

Note that
z_{i}
are the common Casimir functions of
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
, and that
C_{\lambda}(s)
does not depend on
z_{i}
. Compare with Theorem
5.12
. The next example clarifies Theorem
6.9
.

Example 6.11
.

Consider a Poisson pencil on
\mathbb{C}^{3}(x,y,z)
with matrices:

\mathcal{A}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&\lambda_{0}(x,y,z)&0\\
-\lambda_{0}(x,y,z)&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{matrix}\right),\qquad\mathcal{B}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&1&0\\
-1&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{matrix}\right).

Let us consider 3 possible cases:

1.

\lambda_{0}
is locally const. Then the pencil is flat. (And it obviously decomposes.)

2.

\lambda_{0}=\lambda_{0}(z)
. In other words,
\lambda_{0}
is not constant but it is constant on symplectic leaves
z=z_{0}
. In this case the pencil does not decompose, it cannot be a product of its Jordan and Kronecker parts.

3.

\lambda_{0}
depends not only on
z
but also on
x
and
y
(i.e., it is not constant on symplectic leaves
z=z_{0}
). Then by Theorem
6.9
the pencil also locally decomposes into a product of its Jordan and Kronecker parts.

Proof of Lemma
6.8
.

Using Theorems
5.9
and
5.12
we reduce the general case to the the case when

•

there is only one eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}
,

•

and all Kronecker blocks of
\mathcal{P}
are trivial
1\times 1
blocks.

If
d\lambda_{0}\not\in\mathcal{K}
, then we have the decomposition from Theorem
6.8
. We get a Jordan (i.e. nondegenerate) Poisson pencil
\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{Jord}}
with one Jordan tuple with multiple maxima. We get a contradiction with Theorem
4.11
. Lemma
6.8
is proved. ∎

6.2.2
Independence of eigenvalues of Lie-Poisson pencil

Let
a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and let
\lambda_{1}(x)
and
\lambda_{2}(x)
be eigenvalues of a Lie-Poisson pencil
\mathcal{A}_{x+\lambda a}
. Roughly speaking, we want to prove that for generic
a
and
x
the differentials of eigenvalues are linearly independant:

d\lambda_{2}(x)\not=c\cdot d\lambda_{1}(x),\qquad c\in\mathbb{C}.

In this section it is convenient to consider eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
as functions of
x
and
a
. Recall that in a neighbouhood of a generic pair
(x_{0},a_{0})
the eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i}(x,a)
are analytic in both
x
and
a
. Formally speaking, we want to prove the following.

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
\lambda_{1}(x,a)
and
\lambda_{2}(x,a)
be two different eigenvalues of a Lie-Poisson pencil
\mathcal{A}_{x+\lambda a}
. Consider an open subset
U\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}
such that
\lambda_{1}(x,a)
and
\lambda_{2}(x,a)
are locally analytic on
U
. Then for a generic pair
(x_{0},a_{0})\in U
we have

\frac{\partial\lambda_{1}}{\partial x}(x_{0},a_{0})\wedge\frac{\partial\lambda_{2}}{\partial x}(x_{0},a_{0})\not=0.

Lemma
6.12
is the combination of the following Corollary
6.14
and Proposition
6.17
.

Linearly dependant differentials of two eigenvalues coincide.

Proposition 6.13
.

Consider a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, take any
a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Let
\lambda_{1}(x)
and
\lambda_{2}(x)
be two eiganvalues of the Lie-Poisson pencil
\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}
that are locally analytic on
U_{0}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. If for any point
x_{0}\in U_{0}
the differentials are linearly dependant

d\lambda_{2}(x_{0})=c\cdot d\lambda_{1}(x_{0}),\qquad c\in\mathbb{C},

then they coincide:

d\lambda_{2}(x_{0})=d\lambda_{1}(x_{0}).

Note that Proposition
6.13
holds for any
a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, not necessary generic. If for a fixed
a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
we have
d\lambda_{2}(x)=d\lambda_{1}(x)
, then
\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{1}+\operatorname{const}
. For various
a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
that constant is function on
a
.

Corollary 6.14
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
6.12
, if

\frac{\partial\lambda_{1}}{\partial x}(x,a)\wedge\frac{\partial\lambda_{2}}{\partial x}(x,a)=0

on
U
, then

\frac{\partial\lambda_{1}}{\partial x}(x,a)=\frac{\partial\lambda_{2}}{\partial x}(x,a)\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\lambda_{2}(x,a)-\lambda_{1}(x,a)=g(a).

Let us study the differential of a characteristic number
d\lambda(x)
for a Lie-Poisson pencil
\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}
. Consider the reduced polynomial (
16
) for
\operatorname{Sing}_{0}
(i.e. union of codimension 1 components of singular set). The eigenvalue
\lambda=\lambda(x)
is a solution of

f_{j}(x-\lambda(x)a)=0

for some
j
. We can easily find
d\lambda(x)
by differentiating this expression with respect to
x
. Namely, we get the following.

Proposition 6.15
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
f(x)
be a non-zero homogeneous polynomial on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
\lambda=\lambda(x)
be a local analytic function on an open subset
U\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
that satisfies
f(x-\lambda(x)a)=0
. Then the following holds:

1.

For any
x\in U
such that
df(x-\lambda(x)a)\not=0
we have

d\lambda=\frac{df(x-\lambda a)}{\langle df(x-\lambda a),a\rangle}.

(38)

2.

For any
x\in U
we have

\langle d\lambda(x),a\rangle=1.

(39)

Note that (
38
) holds for a generic
x\in U
, since
f(x)
is a polynomial. Therefore, (
39
) holds for all
x\in U
.

Remark 6.16
.

(
39
) can also be written as

\lambda(x+ca)=\lambda(x)+c

for small
c\in\mathbb{C}
. Another way to prove this is to note that if
x-\lambda a\in\operatorname{Sing}
, then

(x+ca)-(\lambda+c)a=x-\lambda a\in\operatorname{Sing}.

Proof of Proposition
6.13
.

By Proposition
6.15
, if
d\lambda_{2}(x_{0})=c\cdot d\lambda_{1}(x_{0})
, then

c=c\cdot\langle d\lambda_{2}(x_{0}),a\rangle=\langle d\lambda_{1}(x_{0}),a\rangle=1.

Proposition
6.13
is proved. ∎

Two eigenvalues cannot be equal up to a constant.

Proposition 6.17
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
6.12
, if
\lambda_{2}(x,a)-\lambda_{1}(x,a)=g(a)
, then
\lambda_{2}(x,a)=\lambda_{1}(x,a)
.

We give two proofs of Propositon
6.17
.

First proof of Proposition
6.17
.

Assume that
g(a)\not\equiv 0
.
We want to prove that the singular set
\operatorname{Sing}
contains an open region, that would be a contradiciton. Recall that
x-\lambda_{i}(x,a)a\in\operatorname{Sing}
. Thus, by taking sufficiently small neighbourhoods, we can assume that there are a smooth hypersurface
S_{1}\subset\operatorname{Sing}
and an open region
W\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
such that

y+g(a)a\in\operatorname{Sing},\qquad\forall y\in S_{1},a\in W

and
g(a)\not=0
for
a\in W
. Consider the surface

S_{2}=\left\{g(a)a\quad\bigr|\quad a\in W\right\}.

It is easy to see that
\operatorname{dim}S_{2}\geq n-1
, since the Jacobian for the map
F(a)=g(a)a
is

J_{F}=g(a)\cdot I+a\otimes dg.

On one hand, the singular set contains

S_{1}+S_{2}=\left\{x_{1}+x_{2}\quad\bigr|\quad x_{i}\in S_{i}\right\}\subset\operatorname{Sing},

where
\dim S_{i}\geq n-1
. On the other hand,
\dim\operatorname{Sing}\leq n-1
. It follows that
T_{y_{1}}S_{1}=T_{y_{2}}S_{2}
for all
y_{i}\in S_{i}
, otherwise
S_{1}+S_{2}
would contain an open region. We get that
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are regions of parallel affine hyperplanes:

S_{i}\subset\left\{x\quad\bigr|\quad l(x)+b_{i}=0,\quad l(x)=l_{1}x_{1}+\dots l_{n}x_{n}.\right\}.

But the singular set
\operatorname{Sing}
is a cone, and any region of an affine hyperplane in it must be a region of a linear hyperplane:

b_{1}=b_{2}=0.

All elements
g(a)a\in S_{2}
must satisfy
l(g(a)a)=0
. That means that
g(a)=0
, if
a
is not parallel to
S_{i}
:

l(a)\not=0\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad g(a)=0.

But
g(a)\not=0
and we get a contradiction. Proposition
6.17
is proved. ∎

Second proof of Proposition
6.17
.

It suffices to prove the statement for a generic pair
(x,a)
. Without loss of generality,
\lambda_{i}(x,a)
are local analytic functions in a neighbourhood of
(x,a)
.

•

Each eigenvalue
\lambda=\lambda(x,a)
is a solutions of

f_{j}(x-\lambda(x,a)a)=0

(40)

for some factor
f_{j}(x)
of the reduced polynomial (
16
).

•

Since the polynomial
f_{j}(x)
is homogeneous, we have

\lambda(cx,ca)=\lambda(x,a)

for any
c\not=0
. Thus,

\lambda_{2}(x,a)-\lambda_{1}(x,a)=g(a)\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad g(ca)=0.

•

Differentiating (
40
) with respect to
a
we get

d_{a}\lambda=-\lambda\frac{df(x-\lambda a)}{\langle df(x-\lambda a),a\rangle}

if
df(x-\lambda(x,a)a)\not=0
. In particular, for a generic pair
(x,a)
we get

\langle d_{a}\lambda,a\rangle=-\lambda.

(41)

•

We have

d_{a}\lambda_{2}-d_{a}\lambda_{1}=d_{a}g(a).

Using (
41
) we get

-g(a)=\langle d_{a}g(a),a\rangle.

Note that the right-hand side is a derivative ”in the radial direction”

\displaystyle\langle d_{a}g(a),a\rangle=\sum_{i}a_{i}\frac{\partial g}{\partial a_{i}}.

It vanishes
\langle d_{a}g(a),a\rangle=0
, since
g(ca)=g(a)
. We have proved that
g(a)=0
.

Proposition
6.17
is proved. ∎

7
Mixed case. Realization of JK invariants

In this section we realize some JK invariants that contain both Jordan and Kronecker blocks. Below we consider the JK invariants with one Kronecker block. The JK invariants with several Kronecker blocks can then be realized by Theorem
3.1
. The case of one trivial
1\times 1
Kronecker block was discussed in Section
6
. The idea, how to contruct Lie–Poisson pencils with one Kronecker block, is simple:

•

The matrix of the Lie–Poisson bracket will have the form

\mathcal{A}_{x}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&A_{xs}&A_{xy}\\
-A_{xs}^{T}&A_{ss}&0\\
-A_{xy}^{T}&0&0\end{matrix}\right).

(42)

•

The submatrix
\left(\begin{matrix}0&A_{xy}\\
-A_{xy}^{T}&0\end{matrix}\right)
corresponds to the Kronecker block and it will be the Lie–Poisson bracket from Theorem
3.4
.

•

The submatrix
\left(\begin{matrix}A_{ss}&0\\
0&0\end{matrix}\right)
corresponds to the mantle (i.e. the sum of Jordan blocks and the core) and it will be the Lie–Poisson bracket from Theorem
6.2
.

•

Finally, we take the matrix
A_{xs}
such that the Jacobi identity is satisfied and
\mathcal{A}_{x}
is a Lie–Poisson bracket.

In Sections
7.1
and
7.2
we consider the cases of the Kronecker
3\times 3
and
(2m+1)\times(2m+1)
blocks, where
m>1
, respectively.

7.1
JK invariants with the Kronecker
3\times 3
block

If there is a Kronecker
3\times 3
block, then all JK invariants are possible. With Theorems
3.1
and
3.4
it remains to prove the following statement.

Theorem 7.1
.

The Jordan-Kronecker invariants with one Kronecker
3\times 3
block and any collection of Jordan tuples
J_{\lambda_{s}}(2n_{s1},\dots,2n_{sp_{s}})
(
s=1,\dots N
) can be realized by a Lie algebra with basis
g,h_{1},h_{2},e^{i}_{tj},f^{i}_{tj}
, where
t=1,\dots,N,i=1,\dots p_{s},j=1,\dots,n_{si}
and commutation relations

\begin{gathered}{}[g,h_{1}]=h_{1},\qquad[g,h_{2}]=h_{2},\qquad[g,e^{i}_{tk}]=e_{tk}^{i},\\
[e_{tj}^{i},f_{tk}^{i}]=\begin{cases}e^{i}_{t,j-k},\quad j>k,\\
\alpha_{t}h_{1}+\beta_{t}h_{2},\quad j=k,\end{cases}\end{gathered}

(43)

where
(\alpha_{t},\beta_{t})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}-0
are constants, such that
(\alpha_{t}:\beta_{t})\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}
are pairwise different.

Remark 7.2
.

The eigenvalues of the pencil from Theorem
7.1
are given by (
45
). It is easy to see that for proportional pair
(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})=c(\alpha_{j},\beta_{j})
the eigenvalues are equal
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}
. Therefore, in Theorem
7.1
we demand
(\alpha_{t}:\beta_{t})
to be different elements of
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
.

Let us illustrate Theorem
7.1
for one
2n\times 2n
Jordan block and the Kronecker
3\times 3
block. The Lie-Poisson bracket in the coordinates

(x,p_{1},\dots,p_{n},q_{1},\dots,q_{n},y_{1},y_{2})

has the form

\mathcal{A}_{x}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|cc|cc}0&\hat{p}&0&y_{1}&y_{2}\\
\hline\cr-\hat{p}^{T}&0&P_{n}(\alpha y_{1}+\beta y_{2})&0&0\\
0&-P_{n}^{T}(\alpha y_{1}+\beta y_{2})&0&0&0\\
\hline\cr-y_{1}&0&0&0&0\\
-y_{2}&0&0&0&0\\
\end{array}\right),

(44)

where

P_{n}(z)=\left(\begin{matrix}z&&&\\
p_{1}&z&&\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
p_{n-1}&\cdots&p_{1}&z\end{matrix}\right),\qquad\hat{p}=\left(p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\right).

Remark 7.3
.

Note that Poisson brackets (
44
) have the form (
29
) as in Theorem
5.9
. Below we show that the core
K=\operatorname{span}\left(h_{1},h_{2}\right)
and the mantle
M=\operatorname{span}\left(h_{1},h_{2},e^{i}_{sj},f^{i}_{sj}\right)
.

We add the commutation relation
[g,e^{i}_{sk}]=e_{sk}^{i}
to get a Lie algebra. Other Jordan blocks are “attached” to the Kronecker block in a similar fashion.

Remark 7.4
.

Denote
\mathfrak{g}
the Lie algebra from Theorem
7.1
and
\mathfrak{h}=\operatorname{span}(h_{1},h_{2},e^{i}_{sj},f^{i}_{sj})
. Then
\mathfrak{h}
is an ideal of
\mathfrak{g}
and it is the Lie algebra from Theorem
6.2
. In other words,
\mathfrak{g}
is a one-dimensional extension of
\mathfrak{h}
:

0\to\mathfrak{h}\to\mathfrak{g}\to\mathbb{C}\to 0.

Proof of Theorem
7.1
.

It is not hard to check the Jacobi identity for (
43
). It suffices to check the identity only for the basis vectors.

•

We know that
\mathfrak{h}=\operatorname{span}(h_{1},h_{2},e^{i}_{sj},f^{i}_{sj})
is the Lie algebra from Theorem
6.2
, hence we don’t have to check the Jacobi identity without
g
.

•

It is clear that
\mathfrak{h}_{1}=\operatorname{span}(g,h_{1},h_{2},f^{i}_{sj})
is a Lie algebra, thus we don’t have to check the Jacobi identity without
e^{i}_{sj}
.

•

It remains to check the Jacobi identity for
g,e^{i}_{sj},f^{i}_{sk}
, for other remaining triplets all terms in the identity are zero:

[g,[e^{i}_{sj},f^{i}_{sk}]]+[e^{i}_{sj},[f^{i}_{sk},g]]+[f^{i}_{sk},[g,e^{i}_{sj}]]=[e^{i}_{sj},f^{i}_{sk}]+0-[e^{i}_{sj},f^{i}_{sk}]=0.

Thus, (
43
) indeed defines a Lie algebra. It remains to calculate the Jordan–Kronecker invariants.

1.

We have
\dim\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal{A}_{x+\lambda a})=1
for almost all
\lambda
, thus there is only 1 Kronecker block.

2.

It is easy to see that

\bigoplus_{\lambda-\textrm{ regular }}\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal{A}_{x+\lambda a})=\operatorname{span}\left(h_{1},h_{2}\right),

thus the Kronecker block is
3\times 3
and core subspace

K=\operatorname{span}\left(h_{1},h_{2}\right).

3.

The mantle subpsace is

M=\operatorname{span}\left(h_{1},h_{2},e^{i}_{sj},f^{i}_{sj}\right).

The Jordan blocks for
\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}
and for their restrictions on
M
coincide. For the restriction
\left(\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}\right)\bigr|_{M}
the JK invariants are known from Section
6
(see also Propositions
4.3
and
4.4
). Namely, each subspace
\operatorname{span}\left(e^{i}_{tj},f^{i}_{sj}\right)
, where
i,t
are fixed and
j=1,\dots,n_{tp_{t}}
, correspond to a Jordan
2n_{tp_{t}}\times 2n_{tp_{t}}
Jordan block with eigenvalue

\lambda_{t}=\frac{\alpha_{t}\langle h_{1},x\rangle+\beta_{t}\langle h_{2},x\rangle}{\alpha_{t}\langle h_{1},a\rangle+\beta_{t}\langle h_{2},a\rangle}.

(45)

We get the required JK invariants. ∎

7.2
General mixed case

Let Kronecker indices be
k_{1},\dots,k_{q}
(i.e. the sizes of Kronecker blocks are
(2k_{j}-1)\times(2k_{j}-1)
). Then we can realize
N
Jordan tuples, where
N\leq k_{1}+\dots+k_{q}
, similar to Section
7.1
. By Theorem
3.1
it suffices to realize one Kronecker
(2m+1)\times(2m+1)
block and
d
Jordan tuples, where
d\leq m+1
. We do it in the next Theorem
7.6
.

First, let us illustrate this theorem for one
2n\times 2n
Jordan block and Kronecker
(2m+1)\times(2m+1)
block. The Lie-Poisson bracket in the coordinates

(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},p_{1},\dots,p_{n},q_{1},\dots,q_{n},y_{0},\dots,y_{m})

has the form

\mathcal{A}_{x}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|cc|c}0&P_{s}&0&Y\\
\hline\cr-P_{s}^{T}&0&P_{n}(y_{s})&0\\
0&-P_{n}^{T}(y_{s})&0&0\\
\hline\cr-Y^{T}&0&0&0\\
\end{array}\right),

where

Y=\left(\begin{matrix}y_{0}&y_{1}&&\\
\vdots&&\ddots&\\
y_{0}&&&y_{m}\end{matrix}\right),\qquad P_{n}(y_{s})=\left(\begin{matrix}y_{s}&&&\\
p_{1}&y_{s}&&\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
p_{n}&\cdots&p_{1}&y_{s}\end{matrix}\right).

The eigenvalue
\lambda_{s}
corresponds to the vector
h_{s}
. In order to the Jacoby identity to hold, we take the following matrix
P_{s}
:

•

If
s\not=0
, then all rows of
P_{s}
except for i-th are zeros:

P_{s}=\left(\begin{matrix}0\\
\vdots\\
p\\
\vdots\\
0\end{matrix}\right),\qquad p=(p_{1},\dots,p_{n}).

•

For
s=0
all rows of
P_{0}
are the same:

P_{0}=\left(\begin{matrix}p\\
\vdots\\
p\end{matrix}\right),\qquad p=(p_{1},\dots,p_{n}).

Remark 7.5
.

Note that the functions
y_{i}
are semi-invariants. There exist vectors
\chi_{0},\dots,\chi_{m}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
such that

\left(\begin{matrix}\left\{x_{1},y_{i}\right\}\\
\dots\\
\left\{x_{m},y_{i}\right\}\end{matrix}\right)=y_{i}\cdot\chi_{i}.

The matrices
P_{i}
are then given by

P_{i}=\chi_{i}^{T}p.

Here we regard
\chi_{i}
and
p
as a
1\times m
and
1\times n
vectors (the matrix
P_{i}
is
m\times n
). The Lie–Poisson pencils in this section is a special case of the Poisson pencils constructed in Section
8
(compare with Remark
8.6
).

For
d\leq m+1
Jordan tuples
J_{\lambda_{t}}(2n_{t1},\dots,2n_{ts_{t}})
we can simultaneously add Jordan blocks in a similar way for
h_{0},\dots,h_{d-1}
. In other words, we take the Lie–Poisson bracket of the form

\mathcal{A}_{x}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|ccccc|c}0&P_{0,1}&0&\cdots&P_{d-1,s_{d}}&0&Y\\
\hline\cr-P_{0,1}^{T}&0&P_{n_{1,1}}(y_{0})&&&&\\
0&-P_{n_{1,1}}^{T}(y_{0})&0&&&&\\
\vdots&&&\ddots&&&\\
-P_{d-1,s_{d}}^{T}&&&&0&P_{n_{d,s_{d}}}(y_{d-1})&\\
0&&&&-P_{n_{d,s_{d}}}(y_{d-1})^{T}&0&\\
\hline\cr-Y^{T}&&&&&&0\end{array}\right).

(46)

The next theorem can be proved similarly to Theorem
7.1
.

Theorem 7.6
.

The Jordan-Kronecker invariants with one Kronecker
(2m+1)\times(2m+1)
block and
d
Jordan tuples
J_{\lambda_{t}}(2n_{t1},\dots,2n_{ts_{t}})
(
t=1,\dots d
), where
d\leq m+1
, can be realized by a Lie algebra with basis
g_{1},\dots,g_{m},h_{0},\dots,h_{m},e^{i}_{tj},f^{i}_{tj}
, where
t=0,\dots,d-1,i=1,\dots,s_{t},j=1,\dots,n_{ti}
and commutation relations

\displaystyle[g_{i},h_{0}]=h_{0},\qquad[g_{i},h_{i}]=h_{i},

\displaystyle[g_{i},e^{i}_{0k}]=e_{0k}^{i},\qquad[g_{t},e^{i}_{tk}]=e_{tk}^{i},

\displaystyle[e_{tj}^{i},f_{tk}^{i}]=\begin{cases}e^{i}_{t,j-k},\quad j>k,\\
h_{t},\quad j=k.\end{cases}

Theorem
7.6
also follows from the results of Section
8
(see Theorem
8.1
, Lemma
8.4
and Corollary
8.5
).

8
Realization of JK invariants by Poisson pencils

In this section we prove Theorem
1.4
that all JK invariants with at least one Kronecker block can be realized by Poisson pencils.

•

In Section
8.1
we construct a family of Poisson pencils
\hat{\mathcal{P}}=\left\{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\lambda}\right\}
(that is not linear on
\lambda
and is not defined for some
\lambda
).

•

In Section
8.2
we turn the family
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
into a Poisson pencil
\mathcal{P}
, if some PDEs (
113
) are satisfied.

•

In Section
8.3
we discuss when the pencil
\mathcal{P}
is a Lie–Poisson pencil. In particular, we get the Lie–Poisson pencils from Theorems
7.1
and
7.6
.

•

In Section
8.4
we study the solutions of PDEs (
113
).

•

In Section
8.5
we combine the results of the previous sections and realize JK invariants by Poisson pencils.

8.1
Family of Poisson brackets

We start with a construction of a family of Poisson pencils
\hat{\mathcal{P}}=\left\{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\lambda}\right\}
that is defined for almost all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
. This family is not necessary linear in
\lambda
. The matrix of
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
will have the form similar to (
42
):

\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&A_{\lambda,xs}&A_{\lambda,xy}\\
-A_{\lambda,xs}^{T}&A_{\lambda,ss}&0\\
-A_{\lambda,xy}^{T}&0&0\end{matrix}\right).

•

The idea is to take the matrices
A_{\lambda,ss}
as in Section
7
, but replace
y_{t}
with some functions
f_{t}(y)
.

•

The submatrix
\left(\begin{matrix}0&A_{xy}\\
-A_{xy}^{T}&0\end{matrix}\right)
corresponds to some Lie–Poisson bracket from Remark
3.6
(below, in Sections
8.4
and
8.5
, we will take this submatrix as in Theorem
3.4
, similar to Section
7
).

•

The blocks
A_{\lambda,xs}
will be modified in such a way that
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
becomes a Poisson bracket.

For instance,
\mathcal{A}
will have the form similar to (
46
), i.e.

\mathcal{A}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|ccccc|c}0&G_{0,1}&0&\cdots&G_{d-1,s_{d}}&0&Y\\
\hline\cr-G_{0,1}^{T}&0&P_{n_{1,1}}(f_{1}(y))&&&&\\
0&-P_{n_{1,1}}^{T}(f_{1}(t))&0&&&&\\
\vdots&&&\ddots&&&\\
-G_{d-1,s_{d}}^{T}&&&&0&P_{n_{d,s_{d}}}(f_{d}(y))&\\
0&&&&-P_{n_{d,s_{d}}}(f_{d}(y))^{T}&0&\\
\hline\cr-Y^{T}&&&&&&0\end{array}\right),

(47)

for some matrices
G_{t}
and
Y
.

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},p^{i}_{tj},q^{i}_{tj},y_{1},\dots,y_{r})
, where
t=1,\dots,d,i=1,\dots,s_{t},\,\,j=1,\dots,n_{ti}
be linear coordinates on a vector space
V
. Let

•

b_{w}
and
\alpha_{uw}
be constants for
u=1,\dots,m
and
w=1,\dots,r
,

•

c^{i}_{tj}
and
\delta_{t}
be constants for
t=1,\dots,d,i=1,\dots,s_{t},\,\,j=1,\dots,n_{ti}
,

•

f_{t}(y)
be smooth functions for
t=1,\dots,d
.

Then for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
such that
f_{t}(y)+\lambda\delta_{t}\not=0
the Poisson bracket
\left\{\cdot,\cdot\right\}_{\lambda}
given by the following commutation relations is well defined:

\left\{x_{u},y_{w}\right\}_{\lambda}=\alpha_{uw}\left(y_{w}+\lambda b_{w}\right),\qquad\left\{p^{i}_{tj},q^{i}_{tk}\right\}_{\lambda}=\begin{cases}p^{i}_{t,j-k}+\lambda c^{i}_{t,j-k},\qquad&j\geq k,\\
f_{t}(y)+\lambda\delta_{t},\qquad&j=k,\end{cases}

(48)

and

\left\{x_{u},p^{i}_{tj}\right\}_{\lambda}=\left(p^{i}_{t,j}+\lambda c^{i}_{t,j}\right)G_{\lambda,ut}(y),

(49)

where

G_{\lambda,ut}(y)=\frac{\left\{x_{u},f_{t}(y)+\lambda\delta_{t}\right\}_{\lambda}}{f_{t}(y)+\lambda\delta_{t}}.

(50)

Note that the Lie–Poisson brackets from Theorems
7.1
and
7.6
are a special case of the brackets from Theorem
8.1
(see also Corollary
8.5
below).

Remark 8.2
.

Note that the right-hand side of (
50
) is a function of
y
and it can be found using (
48
). Therefore, we can calculate (
49
) after we defined the bracket
\left\{x_{u},y_{w}\right\}_{\lambda}
by (
48
).

Proof of Theorem
8.1
.

We need to check the Jacobi indentity.

1.

For the coordinates
(x_{u},y_{w})
we get a Lie–Poisson pencil (see Remark
3.6
) and the functions
q^{i}_{tk}
commute with them. Hence, we don’t have to check the Jacobi identity without
p^{i}_{tk}
.

2.

For the coordinates
(p^{i}_{tj},q^{i}_{tk},y_{w})
we get the Poisson bracket from Proposition
6.3
. Thus, we don’t have to check the Jacobi identity without
x_{u}
.

3.

Note that all functions in the right-hand side of (
48
) and (
49
) are functions of
y
and
p^{i}_{tk}
. Thus, for the triples
(x_{\alpha},p^{i}_{tk},y_{w})
and
(x_{\alpha},p^{i}_{tk},p^{i^{\prime}}_{t^{\prime}k^{\prime}})
all terms in the Jacobi identity vanish. It remains to check the Jacobi identity for following two triples:

(x_{u},p^{i}_{tk},q^{i^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}),\qquad(x_{u},x_{u^{\prime}},p^{i}_{tk}).

4.

Consider the Jacobi identity for
(x_{\alpha},p^{i}_{tk},q^{i^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}})
. Note that

\left\{x_{u},\left\{p^{i}_{tk},q^{i^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\right\}_{\lambda}\right\}_{\lambda}=G_{\lambda,ut}(y)\left\{p^{i}_{tk},q^{i^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\right\}_{\lambda}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\{x_{u},\left\{p^{i}_{tk},q^{i^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\right\}_{\lambda}\right\}_{\lambda}+\left\{p^{i}_{tk},\left\{q^{i^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}},x_{u}\right\}_{\lambda}\right\}_{\lambda}+\left\{q^{i^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\left\{x_{u},p^{i}_{tk}\right\}_{\lambda}\right\}_{\lambda}=

\displaystyle=G_{\lambda,ut}(y)\left\{p^{i}_{tk},q^{i^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\right\}_{\lambda}+0+G_{\lambda,ut}(y)\left\{q^{i^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}},p^{i}_{tk}\right\}_{\lambda}=0.

5.

It remains to prove the Jacobi identity for
(x_{u},x_{u^{\prime}},p^{i}_{tk})
. We get

\displaystyle\left\{x_{u},\left\{x_{u^{\prime}},p^{i}_{tk}\right\}_{\lambda}\right\}_{\lambda}+\left\{x_{u^{\prime}},\left\{p^{i}_{tk},x_{u}\right\}_{\lambda}\right\}_{\lambda}+\left\{p^{i}_{tk}\left\{x_{u},x_{u^{\prime}}\right\}_{\lambda}\right\}_{\lambda}=

\displaystyle=\left\{x_{u},\left(p^{i}_{tk}+\lambda c^{i}_{tk}\right)G_{\lambda,u^{\prime}t}(y)\right\}_{\lambda}-\left\{x_{u^{\prime}},\left(p^{i}_{tk}+\lambda c^{i}_{tk}\right)G_{\lambda,ut}(y)\right\}_{\lambda}+0

By the Leibniz rule the expression takes the form

\displaystyle\left(p^{i}_{tk}+\lambda c^{i}_{tk}\right)\left(\left\{x_{u},G_{\lambda,u^{\prime}t}(y)\right\}_{\lambda}+G_{\lambda,ut}(y)G_{\lambda,u^{\prime}t}(y)-G_{\lambda,ut}(y)G_{\lambda,u^{\prime}t}(y)-\left\{x_{u^{\prime}},G_{\lambda,ut}(y)\right\}_{\lambda}\right)=

\displaystyle=\left(p^{i}_{tk}+\lambda c^{i}_{tk}\right)\left(\left\{x_{u},G_{\lambda,u^{\prime}t}(y)\right\}_{\lambda}-\left\{x_{u^{\prime}},G_{\lambda,ut}(y)\right\}_{\lambda}\right)

It remains to prove that

\left\{x_{u},G_{\lambda,u^{\prime}t}(y)\right\}_{\lambda}-\left\{x_{u^{\prime}},G_{\lambda,ut}(y)\right\}_{\lambda}=0.

Denote

H_{\lambda,t}(y)=f_{t}(y)+\lambda\delta_{t}.

Then

G_{\lambda,ut}(y)=\frac{\left\{x_{u},H_{\lambda,t}(y)\right\}_{\lambda}}{H_{\lambda,t}(y)}.

The expression takes the form

\displaystyle\left\{x_{u},\frac{\left\{x_{u^{\prime}},H_{\lambda,t}\right\}_{\lambda}}{H_{\lambda,t}}\right\}_{\lambda}-\left\{x_{u^{\prime}},\frac{\left\{x_{u},H_{\lambda,t}\right\}_{\lambda}}{H_{\lambda,t}}\right\}_{\lambda}=

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{H_{\lambda,t}}\left(\left\{x_{u},\left\{x_{u^{\prime}},H_{\lambda,t}\right\}_{\lambda}\right\}_{\lambda}-\left\{x_{u^{\prime}},\left\{x_{u},H_{\lambda,t}\right\}_{\lambda}\right\}_{\lambda}\right)-

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{H_{\lambda,t}^{2}}\left(\left\{x_{u},H_{\lambda,t}\right\}_{\lambda}\left\{x_{u^{\prime}},H_{\lambda,t}\right\}_{\lambda}-\left\{x_{u^{\prime}},H_{\lambda,t}\right\}_{\lambda}\left\{x_{u},H_{\lambda,t}(y)\right\}_{\lambda}\right).

The second term is zero. We have already proved the Jacobi indentity in the coordinates
(x_{u},y_{w})
. Therefore, the first term also vanishes:

\left\{x_{u},\left\{x_{u^{\prime}},H_{\lambda,t}(y)\right\}_{\lambda}\right\}_{\lambda}-\left\{x_{u^{\prime}},\left\{x_{u},H_{\lambda,t}(y)\right\}_{\lambda}\right\}_{\lambda}=\left\{H_{\lambda,t}(y),\left\{x_{u},x_{u^{\prime}}\right\}_{\lambda}\right\}_{\lambda}=0.

Theorem
8.1
is proved. ∎

8.2
Construction of Poisson pencils

Consider the family
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
of Poisson brackets from Theorem
8.1
. We want to turn it into a Poisson pencil
\mathcal{P}
. A priori, we have the following problems:

1.

The family
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
is not linear on
\lambda
because of the right-hand side of (
49
).

2.

The bracket
\left\{\cdot,\cdot\right\}_{\infty}
, which should be the constant bracket
\mathcal{A}_{a}
, is not defined.

In Lemma
8.3
we show that we can solve these problems if the functions
f_{t}(y)
satisfies some system of PDE (
113
). Let us intoduce several notations.

•

Denote

\lambda_{t}(y)=\frac{f_{t}(y)}{\delta_{t}}.

(51)

This functions
\lambda_{t}
would be eigenlvalues of the Poisson pencil
\mathcal{P}
.

•

Consider a linear space with coordinates
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},y_{1},\dots,y_{r})
. Denote by

\mathcal{Q}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{y}+\lambda\mathcal{B}_{b}\right\}

the Poisson pencil given by

\left\{x_{u},y_{w}\right\}_{\lambda}=\alpha_{uw}\left(y_{w}+\lambda b_{w}\right).

(52)

Note that this a Lie–Poisson pencil of some Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}
(see Remark
3.6
).

Lemma 8.3
.

Consider the family
\hat{\mathcal{P}}=\left\{\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}\right\}
of Poisson brackets from Theorem
8.1
. Assume that the functions
\lambda_{t}(y)
given by (
51
) satisfy

\left(\mathcal{A}_{y}-\lambda_{t}(y)\mathcal{B}_{b}\right)d\lambda_{t}(y)=0,

(53)

where the pencil
\mathcal{Q}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{y}+\lambda\mathcal{B}_{b}\right\}
is given by (
52
). Then the brackets from
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
have the form

\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}=\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B},

and the family
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
extend to a pencil of compatible Poisson brackets

\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\,\,\bigr|\,\,\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}\right\}.

(54)

Proof of Lemma
8.3
.

Condition (
113
) can be written as

\left\{g(x,y),\lambda_{t}(y)\right\}_{\mathcal{A}_{y}}=\lambda_{t}(y)\left\{g(x,y),\lambda_{t}(y)\right\}_{\mathcal{B}_{b}}

(55)

for any smooth functions
g(x,y)
. The brackets
\mathcal{A}_{y}+\lambda\mathcal{B}_{b}
are the restrictions of the brackets
\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}
to the coordinates
(x,y)
. Thus,

\left\{g,\lambda_{t}\right\}_{\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}}=\left\{g,\lambda_{t}\right\}_{\mathcal{A}_{y}}+\lambda\left\{g,\lambda_{t}\right\}_{\mathcal{B}_{b}}.

(56)

Consider (
50
), it takes the form

G_{\lambda,ut}(y)=\frac{\left\{x_{u},f_{t}(y)+\lambda\delta_{t}\right\}_{\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}}}{f_{t}(y)+\lambda\delta_{t}}=\frac{\left\{x_{u},\lambda_{t}(y)\right\}_{\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}}}{\lambda_{t}(y)+\lambda}.

In the last equality we excluded
\lambda\delta_{t}
from the numerator, since it is a constant. By (
55
) and (
56
) we get

G_{\lambda,ut}(y)=\frac{(\lambda_{t}(y)+\lambda)\cdot\left\{x_{u},\lambda_{t}(y)\right\}_{\mathcal{B}_{b}}}{\lambda_{t}(y)+\lambda}=\left\{x_{u},\lambda_{t}(y)\right\}_{\mathcal{B}_{b}}.

(57)

Therefore, the right-hand side of (
49
) and the pencil
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
are linear on
\lambda
. Thus, we can extend
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
to a Poisson pencil (
54
). Lemma
8.3
is proved. ∎

8.3
Construction of Lie–Poisson pencils

Lemma
8.3
allows us to construct Poisson pencils if we have functions
\lambda_{t}(y)
that satisfy (
113
). The next obvious question is how to find such functions
\lambda_{t}(y)
. We want to realize JK invariants with
N
Jordan tuples and a Kronecker block by Poisson pencils. The idea is to find (
113
) with at least
N
solutions
\lambda_{t}(y)
. We do it in the next section. In this section we construct Lie–Poisson pencils using linear solutions of (
113
). The next statement generalize Lie–Poisson pencils from Theorems
7.1
and
7.6
. Recall that in these theorems the eigenvalues
\lambda_{t}(y)
and functions
f_{t}(\lambda)
are linear semi-invariants.

Lemma 8.4
.

Consider the family
\hat{\mathcal{P}}=\left\{\mathcal{P}_{\lambda}\right\}
of Poisson brackets from Theorem
8.1
. Let
\mathfrak{h}
be the Lie algebra with coordinates
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},y_{1},\dots,y_{r})
and the Lie-Poisson bracket

\left\{x_{u},y_{w}\right\}=\alpha_{uw}y_{w}.

If the functions
f_{t}(y)
are semi-invariants of
\mathfrak{h}
and
\delta_{t}=f_{t}(b)
, then the functions
\displaystyle\lambda_{t}(y)=\frac{f_{t}(y)}{\delta_{t}}
satisfy (
113
).

Proof of Lemma
8.4
.

We show that
\displaystyle\lambda_{t}(y)=\frac{f_{t}(y)}{\delta_{t}}
satisfies (
113
) in Lemma
9.11
below (for linear semi-invariants it also follows from Lemma
9.7
below). ∎

Corollary 8.5
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
8.4
, we can extend
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
to a Poisson pencil
\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right\}
. If
all
f_{t}(y)
are linear semi-invariants, then
\mathcal{P}
is a Lie–Poisson pencil for some Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.

Proof of Corollary
8.5
.

We can extend
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
to
\mathcal{P}
by Lemma
8.3
. It is easy to see that
\mathcal{A}
is linear in
x
and
\mathcal{B}_{b}
is a constant Poisson bracket. Note that all the functions
G_{\lambda,ut}(y)
are constants, since by (
57
)

G_{\lambda,ut}(y)=\left\{x_{u},\lambda_{t}(y)\right\}_{\mathcal{B}_{b}},

(58)

where
\lambda_{t}(y)
are linear functions and
\mathcal{B}_{b}
is a constant bracket. Since
\mathcal{A}
is linear, it is a Lie–Poisson bracket of some Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. It is also easy to see that
\mathcal{B}
is
\mathcal{A}
at some point of
\mathfrak{g}
(the constants
c^{i}_{tj}
and
b_{w}
are the values of the coordinates
p^{i}_{tj}
and
y_{w}
). Thus,
\mathcal{P}
is a Lie–Poisson pencil of
\mathfrak{g}
. Corollary
8.5
is proved.
∎

Remark 8.6
.

In Corollary
8.5
, since
f_{t}(y)
are linear semi-invariants, there are vectors
\chi_{t}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
such that

\left(\begin{matrix}\left\{x_{1},y_{i}\right\}_{\mathcal{A}_{y}}\\
\dots\\
\left\{x_{m},y_{i}\right\}_{\mathcal{A}_{y}}\end{matrix}\right)=y_{i}\cdot\chi_{i}.

It is easy to check that (
58
) takes the form

G_{\lambda,ut}(y)=\left\{x_{u},\frac{f_{t}(y)}{f_{t}(b)}\right\}_{\mathcal{B}_{b}}=(\chi_{t})_{u}.

Thus, if the Lie–Poisson bracket has the form (
47
), then the block
G_{t-1,i}
, given by (
49
), has the form

G_{t-1,i}=\chi_{t}^{T}p^{i}_{t}.

Here we regard
\chi_{t}
as a
1\times m
vector, and

p^{i}_{t}=\left(p^{i}_{t1},\dots,p^{i}_{tn_{ti}}\right)

is a
1\times n_{ti}
vector (the block
G_{t-1,i}
is
m\times n_{ti}
). Note that for Lie–Poisson pencils from Theorems
7.1
and
7.6
we get the matrices that were described in Section
7.2
(Compare with Remark
7.5
.)

8.4
Construction of characteristic numbers

In order to realize JK invariants with
N
Jordan tuples and a Kronecker block by Poisson pencils we need to find the pencil
\mathcal{Q}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{y}+\lambda\mathcal{B}_{b}\right\}
such that the equation (
113
) has at least
N
solutions
\lambda_{t}(y)
. Let the pencil
\mathcal{Q}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{y}+\lambda\mathcal{B}_{b}\right\}
be the Lie–Poisson pencil from Theorem
3.4
. In other words,

\left\{\mathcal{A}_{y}+\lambda\mathcal{B}_{b}\right\}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&Y_{\lambda}\\
-Y_{\lambda}^{T}&0\end{matrix}\right),\qquad Y_{\lambda}=\left(\begin{matrix}y_{0}+\lambda b_{0}&y_{1}+\lambda b_{1}&&\\
\vdots&&\ddots&\\
y_{0}+\lambda b_{0}&&&y_{m}+\lambda b_{m}\end{matrix}\right).

(59)

The equation (
113
) becomes the following system of first order quasi-linear PDE on
u=\lambda_{t}(y)
:

\left(y_{0}-ub_{0}\right)\frac{\partial u}{\partial y_{0}}+\left(y_{j}-ub_{j}\right)\frac{\partial u}{\partial y_{j}}=0,\qquad j=1,\dots,m.

(60)

Next statement shows that at any point
y
there exist any number
N
of solutions of (
60
) with distinct values
u(y)
.

Theorem 8.7
.

Let
b_{0},\dots,b_{m}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
. For any
u_{0}\in\mathbb{C},(a_{0},\dots,a_{m})\in\mathbb{C}^{m+1}
there exists a holomorphic solution
u=u(y_{0},\dots,y_{m})
of (
60
) such that
u(a_{0},\dots,a_{m})=u_{0}
.

Proof of Theorem
8.7
.

First of all, if
\displaystyle u_{0}=\frac{a_{j}}{b_{j}}
for some
j=0,\dots,m
, then we can take the solution

u(y)=\frac{y_{j}}{b_{j}}.

Below we assume that
\displaystyle u_{0}\not=\frac{a_{j}}{b_{j}}
.

The idea of the proof is simple: since (
60
) is a system of first order PDE, we want to use the method of characteristics. For a systems of PDE we find a solution using the (complex) Frobenious theorem. Let us explain the details.

1.

We use the method of characteristics.
Consider the space
\mathbb{C}^{n+2}(y_{0},\dots,y_{m},u)
and the following
m
holomorphic vector field on it:

X_{j}=\left(y_{0}-ub_{0}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{0}}+\left(y_{j}-ub_{j}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{j}},\qquad j=1,\dots,m.

Consider a holomorphic function

F(y_{0},\dots,y_{m},u)=u-f(y_{0},\dots,y_{m}).

Then

dF=du-\sum_{j=0}^{m}\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{j}}dy_{j}.

It is easy to see that
u=f(y)
is a solution of (
60
) if and only if

\langle dF,X_{j}\rangle=0,\qquad j=1,\dots,m.

(61)

Therefore,
we seach for holomorhpic functions
F(y,u)
such that (
61
) holds and

F(a,u_{0})=0,\qquad\frac{\partial F}{\partial u}(a,u_{0})\not=0.

(62)

The first condition in (
62
) comes from
u_{0}=u(y_{0})
. We need the second condition in (
62
) in order to use the Holomorphic Implicit Function Theorem (see e.g.
[
8
]
) and get the solution
u=f(y)
.

2.

We can easily find two holomorphic 1-forms in
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
that are solutions of

\langle\varphi,X_{j}\rangle=0,\qquad j=1,\dots,m.

(63)

They are

\varphi^{1}=du,\qquad\varphi^{2}=-\frac{dy_{0}}{y_{0}-ub_{0}}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{dy_{j}}{y_{j}-ub_{j}}.

(64)

This forms
\varphi^{1},\varphi^{2}
are linearly independant everywhere on a neighborhoood
V
of
(a_{0},\dots,a_{m},u_{0})\in\mathbb{C}^{m+1}
. Next,
let us use the well-known complex Frobenius theorem
(see, e.g. Appendix 8 in
[
16
]
).

Theorem 8.8
.

Let
\varphi^{1},\dots,\varphi^{r}
be holomorphic
1
-forms defined in a neighborhoood
V
of the origin in
\mathbb{C}^{m}
. If
\varphi^{1},\dots,\varphi^{r}
are linearly independant everywhere on
V
and if

d\varphi^{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{r}\psi^{j}_{k}\wedge\varphi^{k},\qquad j=1,\dots,r,

where each
\psi^{j}_{k}
is a holomorphic
1
-form on
U
, then there exist a smaller neighborhoood
W
of the origin and holomorphic functions
g^{1},\dots,g^{r}
on
W
such that

\varphi^{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{r}p^{j}_{k}dg^{k},\qquad j=1,\dots,r

where each
p^{j}_{k}
is a holomorphic function on
W
.

It is easy to see that for the forms
\varphi^{i}
, given by (
64
), we have

d\varphi^{1}=d^{2}u=0,\qquad d\varphi^{2}=\psi\wedge du.

Thus, we can apply the complex Frobenious theorem. We get that

du=p^{1}_{1}dg^{1}+p^{1}_{2}dg^{2},\qquad\varphi^{2}=p^{2}_{1}dg^{1}+p^{2}_{2}dg^{2}.

Without loss of generality
p^{1}_{1}(a_{0},\dots,a_{m},u_{0})\not=0
and we can replace
g^{1},g^{2}
with the holomorphic functions
u
and
g=g^{2}
. We get that

\varphi^{2}=p^{1}(y,u)du+p^{2}(y,u)dg(y,u).

Note that
\varphi^{1}=du,\varphi^{2}
are linear combinations of
du,dg
. Since
\varphi^{i}
are solutions of (
63
) and they are linearly independant (everywhere on
W
), the
1
-forms
du,dg
are also linearly independant (everywhere on
W
) and satisfy (
63
). Therefore,
any holomorphic function

F(y,u)=H(u,g(y,u))

is a solution of (
62
)
.

3.

Consider the following holomorhic functions

F(y,u)=C(u-u_{0})+\left(g(y,u)-g(a,u_{0})\right),

for some
C\in\mathbb{C}
. Any such function
F(y,u)
is a solution of (
62
) and it satisfies

F(a,u_{0})=0.

Note that

\frac{\partial F}{\partial u}(a,u_{0})=C+\frac{\partial g}{\partial u}(a,u_{0}).

Thus for some
C\in\mathbb{C}
we have

\frac{\partial F}{\partial u}(a,u_{0})\not=0.

Using the Holomorphic Implicit Function Theorem we get the required solution
u=u(y)
.

Theorem
8.7
is proved. ∎

8.5
Proof of Theorem
1.4

It suffices to realize JK invariants with one Kronecker block, then we can take the product with the Kronecker Lie–Poisson pencil from Theorem
3.4
(see Remark
3.2
). Let the Jordan tuples be
J_{\lambda_{t}}(2n_{t1},\dots,2n_{ts_{t}})
(
t=1,\dots d
), and the Kronecker size be
(2m+1)\times(2m+1)
.

•

Consider the family of Poisson brackets
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
from Theorem
8.1
, where the pencil
\mathcal{Q}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{y}+\lambda\mathcal{B}_{b}\right\}
, given by (
52
), is the Lie–Poisson pencil from Theorem
3.4
. In other words
\mathcal{Q}
has the form (
59
).

•

By Theorem
8.7
for any point
a\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
there exist
d
solutions
\lambda_{t}(y)
of (
113
) with distinct values
\lambda_{t}(a)
. Consider a neighborhood
M
of
a
such that the functions
\lambda_{t}(y)
are pairwise distinct.

•

Take constants
d_{t}=1
and functions
f_{t}(y)=\lambda_{t}(y)
. By Lemma
8.3
we can extend the family
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
to a Poisson pencil
\mathcal{P}
on
M
.

•

It is proved similar to Theorem
7.1
that the JK invariants of
\mathcal{P}
are the Jordan tuples
J_{\lambda_{t}}(2n_{t1},\dots,2n_{ts_{t}})
and the Kronecker
(2m+1)\times(2m+1)
block. Note that the eigenvalues
\lambda_{t}(y)
are different by construction.

Theorem
1.4
is proved.

9
General mixed case. Obstruction

Recall that
\operatorname{JK}(\mathfrak{g})
, given by (
14
), is the multiset that contains the following elements:

•

tuples
(2n_{1},\dots,2n_{s})
for each Jordan tuple
J_{\lambda}(2n_{1},\dots,2n_{s})
,

•

Kronecker sizes
2k_{i}-1
.

Definition 9.1
.

The
multiplicity
of a Jordan tuple
J_{\lambda}(2n_{1},\dots,2n_{s})
is the multiplicity of the tuple
(2n_{1},\dots,2n_{s})
in
\operatorname{JK}(\mathfrak{g})
. A Jordan tuple
J_{\lambda}(2n_{1},\dots,2n_{s})
is
unique
if it has multiplicity
1
.

Remark 9.2
.

In
[
5
]
the term “multiplicity of a characteristic number” was used. This is another number, let us explain the difference between these “multiplicities”. Consider a Jordan tuple
J_{\lambda}(2n_{1},\dots,2n_{s})
.

•

In
[
5
]
the multiplicity of the characteristic number
\lambda
as the root of the characteristic polynomial
p_{\mathcal{P}}
was used. That is the number of occurrences of
\lambda
in the complete factorization of
p_{\mathcal{P}}
, and it is equal to
n_{1}+\dots+n_{s}
.

•

The multiplicity of the Jordan tuple
J_{\lambda}(2n_{1},\dots,2n_{s})
is the number of instances the tuple
(2n_{1},\dots,2n_{s})
appears in
\operatorname{JK}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Luckily, below we are mostly interested whether a Jordan tuple is unique or not. So, there should not be much confusion with “multiplicities”.

Example 9.3
.

Consider JK invariants:

J_{\lambda_{1}}(4,2),\qquad J_{\lambda_{2}}(4,2),\qquad J_{\lambda_{3}}(4,2),\qquad J_{\lambda_{4}}(2).

The Jordan tuple
J_{\lambda_{1}}(4,2)
has multiplicity
3
. The Jordan tuple
J_{\lambda_{4}}(2)
has multiplicity
1
, i.e. it is unique.

Theorem 9.4
.

Consider JK invariants of a Lie algebra.

•

Let the Kronecker sizes be
2k_{1}-1,\dots,2k_{q}-1
.

•

Let
J_{um}
be the number
6
6

6

Here the letter
u
in
J_{um}
stands for “unique” and the letter
m
means “multiple maxima”.
of unique Jordan tuples with multiple maxima
J_{\lambda_{i}}(2n_{i1},\dots,2n_{is_{i}}),n_{i1}=n_{i2}\geq n_{ij}
.

If there are no Kronecker
3\times 3
blocks and no more than one Kronecker
1\times 1
block, then

J_{um}\leq k_{1}+\dots+k_{q}.

Theorem
9.4
is proved in Section
9.4
. First, we need to prove some facts about characteristic numbers (=eigenvalues)
\lambda_{i}(x)
and semi-invariants of Lie algebras. The idea of the proof of Theorem
9.4
is quite simple:

•

In Section
9.1
(see Lemma
9.11
) we prove that the eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
of unique Jordan tuples are linear semi-invariants.

•

In Section
9.3
(see Lemma
9.7
) we show that under the conditions of Theorem
9.4
linear semi-invariants are linearly independant.

•

This lead to a contradiction with Lemma
6.8
, which requires
d\lambda_{i}
for Jordan blocks with multple maxima to belong to the
(k_{1}+\dots+k_{q})
-dimensional core
\mathcal{K}
.

In order to prove Lemma
9.11
, we need a well-known fact that

d\lambda_{i}\in\operatorname{Ker}\left(\mathcal{A}-\lambda_{i}\mathcal{B}\right)

for any eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}(x)
of a Poisson pencil
\left\{\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right\}
. That fact is proved in Section
9.2
(see Lemma
9.8
).

9.1
Eigenvalues of unique Jordan tuples are linear semi-invariants

Definition 9.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra and
S(\mathfrak{g})
be its symmetric algebra, that we identify with the algebra of polynomials on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. A non-zero element
f\in S(\mathfrak{g})
is a
semi-invariant with weight

\chi_{f}\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
if

\left\{g,f\right\}_{x}=\chi_{f}(dg)f(x)

(65)

for any other
g\in S(\mathfrak{g})
.

We identify elements
e\in\mathfrak{g}
with the corresponding linear functions on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
:

l_{e}:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathbb{C},\qquad l_{e}(x)=\langle e,x\rangle.

Note that linear semi-invariants
e\in\mathfrak{g}
correspond to one-dimensional ideals in
\mathfrak{g}
:

[u,e]=\chi_{e}(u)\cdot e,\qquad\forall u\in\mathfrak{g}.

Lemma 9.6
.

Consider a Lie-Poisson pencil
\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}\right\}
for a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. If
J_{\lambda}(2n_{1},\dots,2n_{s})
is a unique Jordan tuple, then
\lambda(x)
is a linear semi-invariant:

\lambda(x)=\langle e,x\rangle,\qquad\left\{g,\lambda\right\}_{x}=\chi_{\lambda}(dg)\cdot\lambda(x),

for some
e\in\mathfrak{g}
,
\chi_{\lambda}\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and any
g\in S(\mathfrak{g})
.

Proof of Lemma
9.6
.

Eigenvalues are roots of the reduced polynomial

p_{\mathfrak{g},\operatorname{red}}=f_{1}(x)\cdot\dots\cdot f_{s}(x)

(see Section
2.7.2
). Assume that
f_{j}(x-\lambda(x)a)=0
. By Proposition
2.39
, since
J_{\lambda}(2n_{1},\dots,2n_{s})
is a unique tuple,
f_{j}(x)
is a linear function:

f_{j}(x)=\langle u,x\rangle,\qquad u\in\mathfrak{g}.

Recall that the functions
f_{i}(x)
are semi-invariants. Hence,
\lambda(x)
is also a linear function

\lambda(x)=\frac{\langle u,x\rangle}{\langle u,a\rangle}

and a semi-invariant. Lemma
9.6
is proved. ∎

9.2
Linear semi-invariants and Lie-Poisson pencils

In the proof of Theorem
9.4
we use the following Lemma
9.7
. In Sections
9.2.1
and
9.2.2
we prove more general facts for characteristic numbers of Poisson pencils, and for arbitrary semi-invariants respectively. The facts from Sections
9.2.1
and
9.2.2
are useful, but we don’t need them for Theorem
9.4
.

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra and
l_{e}(x)=\langle e,x\rangle,e\in\mathfrak{g}
be a linear semi-invariant. Then for any
x,a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and any function
g\in C^{\infty}\left(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\right)
we have

l_{e}(a)\cdot\left\{g,l_{e}\right\}_{x}=l_{e}(x)\cdot\left\{g,l_{e}\right\}_{a}.

(66)

Recall that we identify the linear function
l_{e}
with the element
e\in\mathfrak{g}=\left(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\right)^{*}
. If
l_{e}(a)\not=0
, then in terms of the Lie-Poisson pencil
\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}
we can write (
66
) as

e\in\operatorname{Ker}\left(\mathcal{A}_{x}-\frac{\langle e,x\rangle}{\langle e,a\rangle}\mathcal{A}_{a}\right).

Proof of Lemma
9.7
.

If
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
are linear coordinates on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, then by the Leibniz rule

\left\{g,l_{e}\right\}_{x}=\sum_{i}\frac{\partial g}{\partial x_{i}}\left\{x_{i},l_{e}\right\}_{x},\qquad\left\{g,l_{e}\right\}_{a}=\sum_{i}\frac{\partial g}{\partial x_{i}}\left\{x_{i},l_{e}\right\}_{a}.

Therefore, it suffices to prove (
66
) for linear functions
g(x)=g_{u}(x)=\langle u,x\rangle,u\in\mathfrak{g}
. Since
l_{e}(x)
is a semi-invariant,

\left\{g_{u},l_{e}\right\}_{x}=\chi_{e}(u)l_{e}(x).

(67)

Since
g_{u}
and
l_{e}
are linear functions on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
,

\left\{g_{u},l_{e}\right\}_{x}=\langle x,[u,e]\rangle,\qquad\left\{g_{u},l_{e}\right\}_{a}=\langle a,[u,e]\rangle.

We get that

\left\{g_{u},l_{e}\right\}_{a}=\chi_{e}(u)l_{e}(a).

(68)

(
66
) follows from (
67
) and (
68
). Lemma
9.7
is proved. ∎

9.2.1
Differential of a characteristic number

Let us prove an important fact about eigenvalues of Poisson pencils on a manifold
M
.

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right\}
be a Poisson pencil on a manifold
M
. For any JK-regular point
x\in(M,\mathcal{P})
and any finite eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}(x)<\infty
we have

(\mathcal{A}-\lambda_{i}(x)\mathcal{B})d\lambda_{i}(x)=0.

(69)

In terms of the Schouten–Nijenhuis bracket the formula (
69
) can also be written as

\left[\mathcal{A}-\lambda_{i}(x)\mathcal{B},\lambda_{i}(x)\right]=0.

Remark 9.9
.

Although we were not able to find the statement of Lemma
9.8
in the literature,
it is well-known to the experts in the field. For nondegenerate pencils Lemma
9.8
follows from a similar statement about eigenvalues of Nijenhuis operators (see Proposition 2.3 from
[
3
]
). For the proof of that fact for Lie-Poisson pencils see, e.g., Proposition 5.2 of
[
13
]
. We also prove a similar statement for semi-invariants of Lie algebras in Section
9.2.2
.

Proof of Lemma
9.8
.

Fix a JK-regular point
x_{0}
and denote
\lambda_{i}(x_{0})=\lambda_{0}
. Since
x_{0}
is regular, the rank of the pencil
\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right\}
is constant in a neighborhood
Ox_{0}
. Notice that
\lambda_{0}
is an eigenvalue at
x\in Ox_{0}
iff

\operatorname{rk}\left(\mathcal{A}-\lambda_{0}\mathcal{B}\right)\bigr|_{x}<\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{P}.

Let
S
be the symplectic leaf of
\mathcal{A}-\lambda_{0}\mathcal{B}
through
x_{0}
. Then

\operatorname{dim}S=\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathcal{A}-\lambda_{0}\mathcal{B}\right)\bigr|_{x}=\operatorname{rk}\left(\mathcal{A}-\lambda_{0}\mathcal{B}\right)\bigr|_{x}

for any
x\in S
. Thus
\lambda_{0}
is an eigenvalue on
S
. Since locally eigenvalues are distinct,
\lambda_{i}(x)=\lambda_{0}
on
S
. We get that
d\lambda_{i}(x)=0
on
T_{x}S=\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathcal{A}-\lambda_{0}\mathcal{B}\right)\bigr|_{x}
for
x\in S
. Thus,

d\lambda_{i}(x_{0})\in\left(\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathcal{A}-\lambda_{0}\mathcal{B}\right)\bigr|_{x_{0}}\right)^{0}=\operatorname{Ker}\left(\mathcal{A}-\lambda_{0}\mathcal{B}\right)\bigr|_{x_{0}},

which proves (
69
) and Lemma
9.8
. ∎

As a consequence of Lemma
9.8
we also get the well-known fact that the eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}(x)
are in involution w.r..t all the brackets
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
. It suffices to use the JK theorem and note that the subspaces
\operatorname{Ker}\left(\mathcal{A}-\lambda_{i}(x)\mathcal{B}\right)
are pairwise othogonal (w.r.t. all forms
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
).

Corollary 9.10
.

In a neighbourhood of a JK-regular point
x
the eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}(x)
of a Poisson pencil
\mathcal{P}=\left\{\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{B}\right\}
are in involution w.r.t. all forms
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}
:

\left\{\lambda_{i}(x),\lambda_{j}(x)\right\}_{\lambda}=0,\qquad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}.

9.2.2
Roots of semi-invariants

Let
f(x)
be a semi-invariant with weight
\chi_{f}
on
\mathfrak{g}
and
a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. In this section we consider the shift of the semi-invariant
f(x-\lambda a)
and study the roots of the equation

f(x-\lambda(x)a)=0.

First, let us show that a formular similar to (
65
) holds for
f(x-\lambda a)
and the bracket
\left\{\cdot,\cdot\right\}_{x-\lambda a}
. We can replace in (
65
) the point
x
with
x-\lambda a
and get

\left\{g(x-\lambda a),f(x-\lambda a)\right\}_{x-\lambda a}=\chi_{f}(dg(x-\lambda a))f(x-\lambda a).

If we put
h(x)=g(x-\lambda a)
, we get

\left\{h(x),f(x-\lambda a)\right\}_{x-\lambda a}=\chi_{f}(dh(x))f(x-\lambda a).

(70)

Formula (
70
) can also be written as

\langle x-\lambda a,[dh(x),df(x-\lambda a)]\rangle=\chi_{f}(dh(x))f(x-\lambda a).

(71)

The next statement generalizes Lemma
9.7
.

Lemma 9.11
.

Let
f(x)
be a semi-invariant on
\mathfrak{g}
and
a\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Assume that
\lambda(x)
is a solution of
f(x-\lambda(x)a)=0
, which is locally analytic on
U\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Then for any function
g(x)
and any point
x\in U
we have

\left\{\lambda,g\right\}_{x}=\lambda\left\{\lambda,g\right\}_{a}.

(72)

Note that in terms of Poisson brackets (
72
) is the formula (
69
).

Proof of Lemma
9.11
.

Let us use the explicit formulas for the Lie-Poisson and frozen argument brackets:

\left\{\lambda,g\right\}_{x}=\langle x,[d\lambda,dg]\rangle,\qquad\left\{\lambda,g\right\}_{a}=\langle a,[d\lambda,dg]\rangle.

Then

\left\{\lambda,g\right\}_{x}-\lambda\left\{\lambda,g\right\}_{a}=\langle x-\lambda a,[d\lambda,dg]\rangle.

For a generic
x\in U
we have
df(x-\lambda a)\not=0
, thus we can use formular (
38
) for
d\lambda
. We get

\left\{\lambda,g\right\}_{x}-\lambda\left\{\lambda,g\right\}_{a}=\frac{1}{\langle df(x-\lambda a),a\rangle}\langle x-\lambda a,[df(x-\lambda a),dg(x)]\rangle.

Using (
71
) we get

\left\{\lambda,g\right\}_{x}-\lambda\left\{\lambda,g\right\}_{a}=-\frac{1}{\langle df(x-\lambda a),a\rangle}\chi_{f}(dg(x))f(x-\lambda a)=0.

The last equality holds since
f(x-\lambda a)=0
, by the definition of
\lambda
. Lemma
9.11
is proved. ∎

9.3
Independance of linear semi-invariants

Lemma 9.12
.

Let
e_{1},\dots,e_{N}
be linear semi-invariants of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
that are pairwise not proportional
e_{j}\not=ce_{i},c\in\mathbb{C}
. If in the JK invariants of
\mathfrak{g}
there are no Kronecker
3\times 3
blocks and no more than one Kronecker
1\times 1
block, then
e_{1},\dots,e_{N}
are linearly independant.

Proof of Lemma
9.12
.

The proof is in several steps.

1.

Without loss of generality
e_{1},\dots,e_{k}
are linearly independent and

e_{k+1}=c_{1}e_{1}+\dots+c_{k}e_{k},

(73)

where all
c_{i}\not=0
. Note that
k\geq 2
, since
e_{j}\not=ce_{i}
.

2.

Each
e_{i}
is a semi-invariant:

[u,e_{i}]=\chi_{i}(u)\cdot e_{i}.

From (
73
) we get

\chi_{1}=\chi_{2}=\dots=\chi_{k+1}.

(74)

3.

Extend
e_{1},\dots,e_{k}
to a basis
g_{1},\dots,g_{n-k},e_{1},\dots,e_{k},
of
\mathfrak{g}
. Let
(y_{1},\dots,y_{n-k},x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
be the corresponding coordinates. Then the Lie-Poisson bracket has the form

\mathcal{A}_{x}=\left(\begin{matrix}*&M_{x}\\
-M_{x}^{T}&0\end{matrix}\right),\quad M_{x}=\left(\begin{matrix}\alpha_{1}x_{1}&\dots&\alpha_{1}x_{k}\\
\alpha_{2}x_{1}&\dots&\alpha_{2}x_{k}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\alpha_{n-k}x_{1}&\dots&\alpha_{n-k}x_{k}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}\alpha_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\alpha_{n-k}\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}x_{1}&\cdots&x_{k}\end{matrix}\right).

(75)

The form of
M_{x}
is due to (
74
).

4.

Note that for (
75
) we have
\operatorname{rk}M_{x}=1
. It is easy to see that

\operatorname{dim}\left(\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{A}_{x+\lambda a}\bigcap\operatorname{Ker}\mathcal{A}_{x+\mu a}\right)\geq k-2

for any
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{C},\lambda\not=\mu
. By Proposition
2.10
there are at least
k-2
Kronecker
1\times 1
blocks. By condition, there are no more than one Kronecker
1\times 1
block. Thus,
k=2
or
k=3
.

5.

Consider the case
k=3
. Note that
\chi_{i}\not=0
, i.e.
\left(\begin{matrix}\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n-k}\end{matrix}\right)\not=0
. Otherwise, there would be at least
3
trivial Kronecker
1\times 1
blocks, which is forbidden. We claim that there are two Kronecker blocks with sizes not bigger than
3\times 3
. Consider solutions
v(\lambda)
of the equation

\left(\mathcal{A}_{x}+\lambda\mathcal{A}_{a}\right)v(\lambda)=0

(76)

that are polynomial in
\lambda
. There are solutions of the form

v(\lambda)=\left(\begin{matrix}0_{n-k}\\
w(\lambda)\end{matrix}\right),

where
w(\lambda)
is the solution of

\left(M_{x}+\lambda M_{a}\right)w(\lambda)=0.

This equation is equivalent to

(x+\lambda a)w(\lambda)=0,\qquad x,a\in\mathbb{C}^{3}.

(77)

Proposition 9.13
.

For a generic pair
(x,a)\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\times\mathbb{C}^{3}
there are solutions
w_{1}(\lambda)
and
w_{2}(\lambda)
of (
77
) such that

\deg w_{1}(\lambda)=0,\qquad\deg w_{2}(\lambda)=1

and
w_{1}(0)
and
w_{2}(0)
are linearly independant.

Proof of Proposition
9.13
.

For any matrix
D\in\operatorname{GL}(3)
we have

(x+\lambda a)w(\lambda)=(x+\lambda a)DD^{-1}w(\lambda).

Since the pair
(x,a)
is generic, without loss of generality, we may assume that

x=(1,0,0),\qquad a=(0,1,0).

For these
x
and
a
we have solutions

w_{1}(\lambda)=(0,0,1),\qquad w_{2}(\lambda)=(\lambda,-1,0).

Proposition
9.13
is proved. ∎

We got two solutions of (
76
) with degrees
0
and
1
such that their initial values are linearly independant. By Corollary
2.23
there are two Kronecker blocks with sizes not bigger than
3\times 3
. We get a contradiction with the statement of the theorem.

6.

Only the case
k=2
left. By Lemma
9.7
for a generic pair
(x,a)
we have

e_{i}\in\operatorname{Ker}\left(\mathcal{A}_{x}-\frac{\langle e_{i},x\rangle}{\langle e_{i},a\rangle}\mathcal{A}_{a}\right).

Note that for a generic pair
(x,a)
we have
\langle e_{i},a\rangle\not=0
, and

\frac{\langle e_{i},x\rangle}{\langle e_{i},a\rangle}\not=\frac{\langle e_{j},x\rangle}{\langle e_{j},a\rangle},

since
e_{i}\not=ce_{j}
. By (
73
) the vectors
e_{1},e_{2}
and
e_{3}
